#5073. Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin traded around $62,640 Thursday, down 0.6% on the day, as Anthropic's record-breaking stock market debut failed to reverse a broader selloff in risk assets.

The cryptocurrency has lost roughly 28% from its late-September peak near $87,000, and doesn’t seem on its way to stop anytime soon.

Anthropic began trading on Nasdaq under ANT after pricing 980 million shares at $83, raising $81.34 billion. Its stock opened at $111.50, up 34%, and briefly pushed the Claude maker's market value past $2 trillion, as Decrypt reported.

The enthusiasm stopped at Anthropic's ticker.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

By 12:18 p.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was down 1.55%, the S&P 500 had lost 1.59%, and the Dow was off 2.03%. Investors remain concerned about expensive AI infrastructure and whether the spending will deliver adequate returns. The weak reaction beyond Anthropic undercut hopes that a blockbuster IPO could revive appetite for beaten-down technology stocks.

Crypto's weakness predates Thursday's listing. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs have shed roughly $2.9 billion this month, including $306.4 million Wednesday, amid rising Treasury yields and expectations of further Federal Reserve rate hikes.

Some traders argue the IPO has diverted capital from crypto, but that remains unproven. As Decrypt previously noted, Bitcoin and AI stocks have declined together, suggesting a wider retreat from risk rather than a simple rotation into Anthropic shares.

Bitcoin's daily chart remains decisively bearish. At around $62,640, BTC trades below both its 50-day and 200-day exponential moving averages (EMAs), with the shorter average beneath the longer one.

This is a bearish alignment commonly associated with an EMA-based death cross. Traditionally, a death cross occurs when the 50-day simple moving average crosses below the 200-day average, signaling deteriorating shorter-term momentum. The EMA variation responds more quickly to recent prices. The crossover was confirmed today.

Momentum reinforces the picture. The relative strength index, or RSI, reads 21.3, well below the 30 threshold traders associate with oversold conditions. That can precede a relief rally, but oversold assets can keep falling.

The average directional index, or ADX, stands at 41.2. ADX measures the strength of a trend, not its direction, with readings above 25 generally indicating a strong trend. Here, the negative directional indicator exceeds its positive counterpart, confirming sellers have the advantage. A high ADX reading alone does not mean a reversal is imminent, even with RSI already deep in oversold territory.

Bitcoin has also been making lower highs and lower lows since September, with November's sharp drop slicing through several support levels. A bullish recovery would first need to reclaim $66,512, followed by resistance around $69,292 and $71,539. Above those levels, $73,785 and then $76,984 would present tougher tests of the prevailing bearish structure.

On the downside, the chart marks immediate support near $62,019, almost directly beneath the current price. A decisive daily close below that level could expose the psychological $60,000 threshold, followed by the chart's next Fibonacci extension around $56,841.

The RSI leaves room for a bounce, but the EMAs and ADX show how much work buyers still face. A move through $66,512 would open a test of $69,292; a close under $62,019 would instead put $60,000 back in focus.

#5072. Investor Bill Miller Says Bitcoin Is Easier to Value than AI

Famous Bitcoin (BTC) advocate and investor Bill Miller IV said that it is much easier to value the world's largest cryptocurrency than AI investments, even though Bitcoin does not produce any cash flow.

He said that this has to do with the expectations investors have when it comes to these two assets, specifically regarding their capacity to deliver future profits.

In a recent X post, Miller, who is the chairman and chief investment officer of Miller Value Partners, broke down why he thinks that it's easier to make an investment case for Bitcoin than for AI companies.

"Bitcoin and AI owners have different expectations. The owners of AI expect companies will one day produce free cash flow. Bitcoin owners never did or will expect this," Miller said.

And the end result is that "the valuation case is still much easier for Bitcoin than AI."

Simply put, when investors buy stocks in AI companies, there is an expectation that at some point these companies will deliver big profits and free cash flow.

Free cash flow is the money a company produces from its operations after capital expenditures. This is a significant metric for understanding the value of a company.

However, when it comes to AI companies, it can be difficult to assign a value because investors need to try to determine what kind of revenue these companies will generate, how much they will invest in infrastructure, and when these investments will start to deliver sufficient returns.

Miller has made it a point to push back against the argument that Bitcoin does not have value because it doesn't produce cash flow.

In a commentary from June 30, Miller explained that the traditional way of looking at intrinsic value, which revolves around future cash flows, doesn't really capture the potential of Bitcoin as a decentralized monetary network.

Instead, Miller said that Bitcoin's value could be determined by comparing its potential role within the global financial system to that of gold.

In his view, Bitcoin could theoretically reach about $1.4 million per coin if it achieved a valuation similar to gold's. At least, that's what he calculated at the time.

#5071. SEC charges 8 firms over fake crypto platforms tied to $37M fraud

The SEC says the operators showed investors fake certificates and pointed them to filings on the agency’s website as proof of regulatory approval.

The US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) charged eight Colorado-based entities Thursday, alleging they used fake crypto trading platforms to take $37.4 million from more than 1,500 US investors.

Four of the defendants posed as investment advisers, and each steered investors to a fake trading platform run by its partner, according to the SEC’s complaint. The four pairs operated in succession from August 2024 to May 2026.

The defendants allegedly lured investors by masquerading as investment advisers or representatives of crypto asset trading platforms, which the SEC described as fronts designed to steal investor money, the agency announced in a Thursday press release.

The SEC said the operators were likely overseas and used social media ads to lure investors into private chat groups. They first offered stock-trading advice before persuading victims to transfer money to fake crypto platforms, where victims believed they were buying genuine crypto assets.

The SEC previously warned investors that scammers used investment-related group chats to promote fraud schemes, including by impersonating financial professionals, according to a December 2025 alert.

The scheme allegedly operated through four successive adviser-platform pairs from August 2024 to May 2026, with operators switching pairs every few months.

The eight entities charged were Advisor WeChat Capital, Tech Brokerage Asset Alliance Ltd., HB Wealth Advisors, IdeaCrypto Exchange LLC, Convergent Wealth Advisors Inc., RealSimple Crypto Exchange Inc., Associated Wealth Advisors Ltd., and Essential Crypto Exchange.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the SEC Enforcement Division’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit, encouraging the public to report such schemes through its online portal.

During fiscal year 2026, the SEC filed 348 enforcement actions, down 23.7% from 456 a year earlier. The SEC filed 13 crypto-related actions in calendar year 2025, down from 33 in 2024, according to Cornerstone Research.

#5070. XRP’s institutional adoption story has a catch that investors may be overlooking

Aviva’s manager or platform pays the ledger fees. Ondo investors can deal in RLUSD, while the accounts responsible for fees or reserves need XRP.

The XRP Ledger Foundation said on Nov. 18 it had assessed the network against all ten principles of the Global Layer One Market Infrastructure Toolkit. It dated an independent report from blockchain security firm CertiK Nov. 13. On Nov. 19, CertiK said it independently checked selected technical controls under Architecture and Resiliency, using evidence that included code, configurations, logs and test results.

Legal basis and settlement finality were part of the Foundation’s own assessment. The GL1 toolkit gives institutions a common set of questions for their own due diligence. The Foundation says the exercise confers no regulatory approval or certification. Both fund designs were in place before the November disclosures.

Aviva handles sterling dealing off-chain and records matching tokens on the ledger. Ondo lets eligible investors deal in RLUSD on the ledger.

The asset investors use to deal is separate from the XRP needed for network fees or reserves.

The routes illustrate product terms. They do not measure trading volume, fees paid over time or XRP balances at specific accounts.

The paths compare issuer-described subscriptions and redemptions with the ledger’s documented fee and reserve requirements.

The routes illustrate product terms. They do not measure trading volume, fees paid over time or XRP balances at specific accounts.

Aviva put £50 million into the tokenized sterling share class it announced on Oct. 26. The approximately £22 billion quoted at launch was the size of the underlying Sterling Liquidity Fund. The smaller figure measures Aviva’s own investment in the class.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Under the fund prospectus, an investor sends a subscription through the administrator, which deals in cash off-chain and records the shares on its register. The platform issues the matching tokens after the subscription money arrives and the shares are issued. On redemption, the administrator processes the order and updates the register before the tokens are burned. The manager reconciles the two records daily. If the share register and the ledger diverge, the prospectus gives the register the final say over legal ownership.

Digital custodian Komainu holds the tokens in wallets for eligible investors. Ordinary investor-to-investor transfers are unavailable under the model. Aviva’s prospectus assigns the ledger transaction fees to the manager or tokenization platform, so investors can use the share class without buying XRP themselves. The fee provision leaves account reserve funding unspecified.

Ondo’s May 2026 bank-payout pilot used a different route. Ripple redeemed some OUSG on the ledger, then Mastercard routed the payout instruction and J.P. Morgan’s Kinexys sent U.S. dollars to Ripple’s bank account in Singapore. Ondo’s disclosures give no figures for routine on-ledger dealing and leave the reserve funder unspecified.

The network destroys XRP used for transaction fees. The documented minimum for a standard transaction is 0.00001 XRP, and it can rise under load. Account reserves work differently. The mainnet base reserve is 1 XRP, and an account typically needs another 0.2 XRP for each eligible ledger object it owns, subject to exceptions. Reserve XRP stays in the balance and can still cover fees. The account responsible for a fee or reserve needs to have the XRP for it.

Evidence of a bigger XRP requirement would come from fund terms that call for XRP settlement or collateral, or from repeated XRP transfers or posted collateral linked to identified fund accounts. Matching those transactions to product terms or settlement records would show what the XRP is doing. A wallet balance could simply keep the account funded for future fees and reserves.

#5069. UK to ban crypto exchanges from making markets on their own platforms as Bitcoin rules change

Firms can make markets in spot Bitcoin on qualifying venues without principal-dealing permission for that activity. UK exchanges face separate rules.

The UK is carving out a role for third-party firms that make markets in spot Bitcoin. From Oct. 25, 2027, they can buy and sell on qualifying crypto trading platforms without permission to deal as principal for that activity. The Financial Conduct Authority’s final rules bar UK platform operators from making markets on their own venues.

HM Treasury made the amending regulations on Nov. 18, 2026, adding Article 9UA to the UK’s regulated activities framework. The provision excludes market making on a qualifying cryptoasset trading platform from Article 9T, which covers dealing in qualifying cryptoassets as principal. A separate part of Article 9UA excludes trading strictly for a firm’s own purposes when it provides no service to anyone else.

A qualifying platform brings together multiple third-party buyers and sellers so their orders can result in trades in qualifying cryptoassets. The exclusion covers market making in those assets, including spot Bitcoin.

In its draft guidance, the FCA describes a market maker as a firm that stands ready to buy and sell listed assets on a continuing basis at prices it sets. The regulator proposes that responding to requests for quotes on the platform can also qualify.

CryptoSlate previously covered how the FCA’s final crypto rules govern venue access and authorization when the new regime begins.

Operating a UK qualifying cryptoasset trading platform requires FCA authorization. The FCA’s final venue rules require operators to identify, document and monitor market-making users and disclose the terms of relevant incentives. A separate affiliate may trade on the venue under the same access rules as other users.

An operator can step between buyer and seller to execute client orders on its own platform as a matched principal when it holds the appropriate additional permission and follows venue rules that give it no discretion over how orders are matched. An operator that executes client orders against its own capital away from its own venue needs principal-dealing permission and must disclose its role and manage conflicts.

The UK rules give distinct treatment to third-party market making, a venue trading for itself and a venue executing client orders from Oct. 25, 2027.

Market making on a qualifying platform is excluded from principal dealing. On its own venue, a UK operator needs separate permission to execute orders as matched principal.

The diagram covers UK platform conduct and the principal-dealing exclusion. Other regulated activities may require separate permissions.

Compare the statutory Article 9UA exclusion with CRYPTO 6’s final rules on UK platform activities.

The diagram covers UK platform conduct and the principal-dealing exclusion. Other regulated activities may require separate permissions.

Firms that take orders from customers have to assess their permissions separately. Dealing as an agent or arranging deals can require permission. Firms executing or forwarding orders covered by the FCA’s rules for UK retail and elective professional clients must use a UK qualifying cryptoasset execution venue.

The FCA’s application window closes Feb. 28, 2027, for eligible firms with an existing UK cryptoasset business that want transitional protection for regulated activities. Market making that falls within Article 9UA carries no principal-dealing application requirement for that activity.

The FCA’s consultation on the carve-out closed Nov. 18, 2026. Its consultation page targets January 2027 for final guidance, while the consultation paper gives spring 2027.

#5068. Hackers Leak Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Customer Data

Hackers Leak Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Customer Data

Three of the biggest names in payments, Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Chase, disclosed that some of their customers’ data was exposed after hackers breached third-party vendors, the latest jolt in a week of tech failures that has left Americans uneasy about the systems underpinning daily life.

The data was leaked Thursday by a ransomware group, according to a report from The Wall Street Journal. Crucially, all three firms said their own networks were not breached and that no payment systems were compromised.

A Visa spokesperson said no payment data such as card numbers was affected and that the incident involved a third-party service provider in the Asia-Pacific region.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Mastercard said a “limited amount of information from a subset of cardholders enrolled in a specific benefit program” was exposed, with no impact on its payment network.

JPMorgan said attackers targeted outside service providers and likely accessed marketing data, potentially including customer names and email addresses, with no disruption to customer services.

A ransomware group calling itself n0n claimed responsibility, according to cybersecurity firm SOCRadar. None of the three companies named the breached vendor.

Earlier in the week, JPMorgan customers hit problems accessing accounts and sending payments, which the bank attributed to a software update, and the issue spread into broader complaints about other banks.

Paired with the FAA temporarily grounding flights and an earlier Microsoft outage that locked people out of email, the week stoked unusual public anxiety about the fragility of critical tech. JPMorgan was explicit that the data leak had “no connection” to the earlier service disruptions.

For crypto users, the breach lands squarely within a growing danger that customer data, not just funds, is the soft underbelly of finance.

AI has supercharged both the scale and sophistication of cyberattacks, and 2026 has brought a steady drumbeat of data exposures. The record $6.3 billion Binance hack, tied to North Korea, laid bare how aggressively state and criminal actors are targeting financial infrastructure, while a wave of breaches has hit crypto firms through third-party vendors, echoing the vendor weakness at the center of this latest incident.

The payments giants have all leaned deeper into crypto and stablecoins this year, from Visa and Mastercard’s settlement programs to JPMorgan’s growing digital-asset footprint, making their security posture increasingly relevant to the onchain economy they’re racing to join.

For now, the firms say the damage is contained, but the episode adds to a mounting sense that no institution, however large, is immune.

#5064. Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick, with Mikhail Kudinov and collaborators, published a security analysis on Thursday for SHRINCS, advancing an emerging frontrunner for a post-quantum signature scheme in Bitcoin.

“We need all the possible feedback—engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said in reference to a draft of SHRINCS’ Bitcoin Improvement Proposal (BIP). “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise."

Notably, SHRINCS is a hash-based signature scheme that leverages SHA-256 to anchor its security in battle-tested primitives that are already used by Bitcoin, representing a conservative effort to minimize the proposal’s trust assumptions.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, the scheme comes with trade-offs, most notably by introducing potential security vulnerabilities at the implementation level if misused. The scheme combines two spending paths: a small, more efficient stateful signature option and a larger, more costly stateless path with fewer security risks on the implementation side.

Although stateful signatures are compact, preserving Bitcoin’s ability to process transactions efficiently, a user can trigger a critical failure—where an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal funds if a one-time state is accidentally reused. Because SHRINCS’ stateful path relies on one-time keys that can only safely sign a single transaction, wallet developers must carefully track each key’s state.

“It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” Nick told Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

According to Nick, for SHRINCS to work safely in the real world, its specification and corresponding wallet implementations must automatically enforce the stateless path fallback in any situation where a state counter could possibly be compromised.

While some users might gravitate toward stateless signatures out of caution, Nick warns that widespread adoption could bloat the blockchain and drive up fees across the network.

Bitcoin’s current ECDSA signatures are typically 71 bytes long (and Taproot Schnorr signatures are 64 bytes). Under SHRINCS, stateful signatures are anywhere from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while stateless ones are 5,777 bytes. That said, optimizations of both signature paths have been accelerating with the support of AI-based research, Nick noted.

“We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

As the SHRINCS working group—which includes researchers backed by nonprofits including Brink and OpenSats—continues to refine the proposal’s specification, Blockstream researchers stress that getting the cryptographic foundation right is only half the battle.

Whether the broader Bitcoin ecosystem is ready to embrace SHRINCS’ inherent trade-offs will depend heavily on feedback from the network’s stakeholders in the months ahead. Meanwhile, Nick and Kudinov are requesting review of the proposal and the security analysis.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#5062. AI just exposed a major weakness in anonymous peer reviews

The model saw one report at a time across 398 reviews and meta-reviews. It scored lower on a larger set of public reviews.

Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5.2 correctly named the writer of 56% of 398 reviews and meta-reviews in a small test of unsigned peer reviews. Six volunteers wrote the reports. The model saw one report at a time, without web access, tools, a list of possible authors or other writing samples.

In a Nov. 19 preprint, Atrey Desai and colleagues analyzed reports written between 2015 and 2026 for 26 double-blind conferences and workshops in machine learning and natural language processing. The researchers knew who wrote each report, but the reviewers’ names had never been publicly linked to them. In 19 cases, the text itself was already publicly available without a name.

Claude named the writer on 68.4% of 266 standard reviews and 31.1% of 132 meta-reviews, which summarize the reviews of a submission. The meta-reviews were shorter and came from just two of the six contributors. The study did not separate the effects of report length, type and authorship.

The 56% result counts each report equally, so contributors who supplied more reports had more influence on the overall figure. When the researchers gave each reviewer equal weight, Claude’s average accuracy was 49.1%. The test showed that an unsigned report can give its writer away. Estimating how often that happens across confidential peer review would take a larger, more varied sample.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

In the study, Claude Fable 5.2 saw one report at a time, without web access, tools, candidate names or extra writing samples.

Only two of the six volunteers wrote the meta-reviews.

The meta-reviews were shorter. The study cannot separate the effects of report length, type and writer.

Claude Fable 5.2 received one text per attempt, without web access, tools, candidate names or additional writing samples. Both bars use a common 0% to 100% scale. The rates are reported in Table 32.

The meta-reviews were shorter. The study cannot separate the effects of report length, type and writer.

The researchers also tested 1,559 public reviews from 47 open-review journals. Claude correctly identified the reviewer in 21.4% of the 1,530 cases it answered. The paper reports 39.3% accuracy for a subset containing 789 reviews that Pangram 4.1, an AI-text detector, had labeled human or mixed. The paper’s abstract cites that filtered rate, rounded to 39%.

Accuracy was higher on public reviews whose writers cited their own work. Claude recorded 80.2% accuracy in the group of 106 reviews with self-citations and 17.1% in the group of 1,453 without them.

Claude made mistakes even in the group where it performed best. An editor or author presented with a name from Claude would still need independent evidence to establish who wrote an unsigned review. CryptoSlate has also reported on how frontier AI models handled fabricated research data. The team says it plans to add results from a substantially larger set of anonymous peer reviews in an update to the preprint.

#5056. Bitcoin nears repeat of chart pattern that preceded 2023’s $25K retest

Bitcoin’s 21-day moving average fell below its 200-day average, and analyst Keith Alan expects a second bearish crossover by Sunday.

Bitcoin (BTC) could repeat a pair of bearish moving-average crossovers that preceded its retest of $25,000 in September 2023, according to analyst Keith Alan.

New analysis from Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, compares this week’s Bitcoin chart with a similar setup in 2023.

On Bitstamp’s BTC/USD daily chart, the 21-day simple moving average (SMA) crossed below its 200-day equivalent on Thursday. The move last occurred in early November 2025, around one month after Bitcoin’s latest all-time high of $126,200, in the early stages of the current bear market. The 50-day SMA crossed below the 200-day SMA later that month, forming a conventional death cross.

Alan expects the 50-day SMA to cross below the 100-day SMA by the end of the week.

“We’ve only seen this happen once before in Bitcoin’s history, and it should not be ignored,” he summarized in a post on X.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21-, 50-, 100- and 200-day SMAs. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The only other time in Bitcoin’s history that the two pairs of moving averages both produced a so-called “death cross” in the same period was between August and September 2023. BTC/USD retested support near $25,000 on Sept. 11.

Alan describes this double bottom as the start of a rally that took Bitcoin to its 2023 high of $44,700 in early December — an increase of 80%.

“One occurrence is by no means a large enough sample to consider a reliable pattern, or expect similar returns/drawdowns, however the timing, placement, and candle characteristics have enough similarities that we can’t ignore it either,” he said.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21-, 50-, 100- and 200-day SMAs. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Previously, Cointelegraph reported on Bitcoin losing support at the 200-week SMA for the first time since mid-August. The trend line typically functions as support during bull-market retracements, but it can act as resistance when Bitcoin rebounds from below it.

Related: Institutions pile back into US Bitcoin ETFs in Q3 after selling streak

BTC/USD fell to $61,231 on Friday, its lowest level since July 3, a day after Anthropic’s record IPO and Nasdaq debut. The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index finished Thursday’s trading session down 2.25% at 23,879 points, its lowest daily close since April 14.

Anthropic shares traded around $91.50 before Friday’s open. It closed Thursday at $97.17, 20% below the day’s $115 peak.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#5054. XRP Ledger trial settled 213,000 paid AI requests in 6,324 transactions

t54 Labs reported an average network fee of 0.36 drops per paid request in its closed October beta. Two November checks paid the same merchant in RLUSD, with individual requests counted off-ledger.

Two checks cashed on the XRP Ledger on Nov. 18 paid the same AI service in RLUSD. Payments company t54 Labs identified both as batches. In its closed October beta, t54 reported that bundling requests cut the average network fee to 0.36 drops of XRP per paid call.

The checks delivered 0.31 RLUSD and 0.46 RLUSD to the same ledger address. The XRPL AI Hub, run by t54 Labs, identifies the merchant as Heurist Inference Router and classifies both transfers as batches.

The first check and second check came from different payer addresses. Each had a spending ceiling of 1 RLUSD. The ledger charged 12 drops of XRP to open each check and 12 to cash it, for a total of 24 drops per pair.

A drop is one millionth of an XRP. The Hub marks payment intent as unverified in its first receipt and its second receipt. The receipts give no call counts, so the November fee per request remains unknown.

t54 Labs reported that its mainnet beta settled 213,008 paid requests across 3,162 sessions using 6,324 ledger transactions from Oct. 14 to 17. It put the average network fee at about 0.36 drops per paid request. Its benchmark was a single transaction per request at the ledger's standard minimum fee of 10 drops, which can rise under load.

In t54's beta, the payer first opens a check on the ledger with a maximum amount. A session key authorizes each charge off-ledger as the agent makes paid requests. At the end of the session, the merchant cashes the check once for the amount used. Each completed session takes two ledger transactions, so adding requests to a session spreads those two fees over more charges.

The beta began on mainnet in October with RLUSD as its only settlement asset. t54 says it is limited to onboarded merchants and registered payers, and its developer guide still describes sessions as part of internal merchant testing. The public facilitator has yet to offer batch sessions, according to t54.

An open check adds 0.2 XRP to the payer's reserve requirement on top of the regular 1 XRP account reserve. That amount remains in the account while the check is open. A payer with several checks open at once needs the extra reserve for each one. The reserve requirement falls when the check is cashed or canceled. Expiration alone leaves the check on the ledger until someone cancels it.

#5053. Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Anthropic made stock market history Thursday, raising $81.34 billion in the largest initial IPO share sale ever. But Wall Street's response was considerably less enthusiastic than the demand for the Claude developer's shares.

The stock opened at $111.50 on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT, a 34% premium over its $83 offering price, briefly pushing the company's fully diluted valuation past $2 trillion.

The rally faded quickly, however, with shares dropping toward $93 before closing today at $97.17.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The offering involved 980 million shares and surpassed SpaceX's original $75 billion IPO in June. As Decrypt previously reported, SpaceX's expanded final offering reached $86.25 billion, meaning Anthropic has not surpassed that completed deal.

Investors initially welcomed the listing, sending technology stocks higher before the opening bell. By the afternoon, however, the Nasdaq Composite was down 2.06%, the S&P 500 had fallen 1.9%, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average was off 2.17%.

The selloff extended across the AI supply chain. A Thursday afternoon market heatmap showed Nvidia falling 3.4%, Broadcom losing 2.07%, and memory chipmaker Micron slipping 2.17%. Microsoft was down 1.97%, while cloud infrastructure and software provider Oracle declined 2.97%.

AMD was among the hardest hit, down 7.73% in the day, although its decline had a separate catalyst. Reuters reported that the chipmaker came under pressure after investigative outlet Hunterbrook disclosed that its affiliated investment fund had taken a short position in the company.

The picture was not uniformly negative. Palo Alto Networks rose 6%, CrowdStrike gained 2.24%, and Intel added 2.25% in the same snapshot. The divergence shows that investors were distinguishing between companies rather than selling every technology stock indiscriminately.

The broader weakness comes as investors question the cost of building AI infrastructure and the returns companies can generate from that spending. Nvidia's latest earnings beat expectations, but uncertainty following Jensen Huang's resignation has continued to weigh on the semiconductor giant.

Anthropic also faces an expensive expansion. Its IPO disclosures detailed approximately $900.9 billion in infrastructure and computing commitments extending through 2045, alongside another $150 billion to $175 billion in estimated payments under less precisely defined arrangements.

"Anthropic's record-breaking IPO has failed to rekindle enthusiasm for tech stocks," Joe Maher, market economist at Capital Economics, told Reuters.

Nvidia's weakness was notable given its latest results. The chipmaker reported $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue on Tuesday, up 103% from a year earlier, and forecast stronger-than-expected sales for the following quarter.

Even those numbers failed to erase concerns over the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang after allegations of extramarital affairs and possible leaks of corporate secrets and the sustainability of the AI spending boom.

The pressure also reached companies that rent out computing capacity. Neocloud operators CoreWeave and Nebius fell 5.0% and 7.8%, respectively. Software companies were weak as well: Salesforce declined 3.17% and Adobe lost 2.03% in the afternoon heatmap. From chips and data centers to enterprise software, the losses extended beyond any single corner of the AI trade.

Anthropic's IPO prospectus shows how closely these companies are tied together. Nvidia and Google each committed roughly $10 billion to a conditional private share placement alongside the IPO.

AMD agreed to invest $2.5 billion; Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft committed $1 billion apiece; and TSMC pledged $500 million. The $26 billion placement is separate from the public offering. Several of these investors are also key suppliers or infrastructure partners in the AI ecosystem.

That creates a central question for investors: How much of the money flowing into AI companies will ultimately translate into profitable demand for chips, cloud capacity and software?

Anthropic's blockbuster debut demonstrates demand for its shares, but Thursday's broader decline shows that Wall Street is not ready to extend that confidence to every company financing or supplying the AI boom.

#5049. Senators want state-licensed firms to keep issuing stablecoins after Jan. 18

Six senators are asking Treasury to let qualifying issuers keep issuing stablecoins while federal regulators review their states’ rules. The GENIUS Act takes effect Jan. 18, 2027.

Six senators are asking Treasury to let qualifying state-regulated stablecoin issuers keep putting new tokens into circulation while their states’ rules are under federal review. The GENIUS Act takes effect on January 18, 2027. Treasury’s proposal recognizes temporary relief for eligible federal applicants that get a waiver. It leaves open what happens if a state’s regulatory framework still awaits certification.

Sens. Cynthia Lummis and Kirsten Gillibrand led the bipartisan November 17 letter, which Lummis’s office released November 18. They asked Treasury for temporary protection during certification for issuers in states that will have a qualifying framework in place before January 18, 2028. They warned that a clearer federal route could steer issuers toward a federal license.

The proposed Section 3 rule would recognize relief for two groups with applications pending when the law takes effect. One is a subsidiary of an insured depository institution whose parent has applied on its behalf. The other is an applicant seeking to become a federally qualified payment stablecoin issuer. The primary federal regulator must grant a waiver, and its terms must cover the relevant Section 3 restriction. Relief can run for up to 12 months from the law’s effective date.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) has a separate final rule that requires a written request and a substantially complete issuer application pending on or before January 18, 2027. It can grant relief on public-interest grounds or in extraordinary circumstances. Its licensing page says it has yet to open applications or waiver requests and is awaiting clearance for its application forms.

Treasury’s proposal treats the first transfer that gives someone outside the issuer the right to use, transfer or redeem the token as issuance. An issuer could create tokens for its own inventory and still face a restriction when it tries to put those tokens into circulation.

The OCC waiver rule gives active issuers a route to request temporary relief before the January deadline.

Treasury asked whether issuers should get relief before a state files for certification or while a review is pending. Four state regulatory and legislative groups asked for such protection in October. New York’s Department of Financial Services offered a different reading. It says qualifying issuers can keep issuing if their state’s rules are substantially similar and the state files within a year, until any final denial. It wants Treasury to confirm that reading in the final rule.

In the preamble to its interim rule, the Stablecoin Certification Review Committee described the state option for issuers with $10 billion or less in consolidated outstanding stablecoin issuance as requiring a state filing and committee approval. The committee has 30 days to decide once a complete certification has been submitted. It will seek to expedite initial reviews after the Act takes effect for states that had a qualifying regime in place by January 14, 2026. New York says it expects to qualify for expedited review. The initial state filing is due within a year of the Act taking effect.

The committee says it will start accepting certifications once the forms clear federal review. The paperwork record still shows the certification forms under review. If Treasury treats committee approval as a prerequisite and offers no temporary relief, issuers that depend on the state route could have to wait to issue new units.

Treasury’s proposal recognizes express federal waivers. It leaves the treatment of issuers under pending state certifications open.

A January pause could affect new supply before the general platform-sales rule applies.

Foreign-issued stablecoins face separate platform requirements from January 18, 2027.

The two branches map the federal waiver in proposed § 1523.4(a) and Treasury’s question about relief while state certification is pending. New York’s legal reading comes from its October 5 comment. The dates are statutory milestones.

Foreign-issued stablecoins face separate platform requirements from January 18, 2027.

New York’s approvals show why the legal issuer matters. The state’s Greenlist and charter register identify Ripple USD (RLUSD) and WisdomTree Dollar (USDW) as approved for issuance in the state. Ripple’s November 16 terms name Ripple Custody & Trust Co., the trade name of New York-chartered Standard Custody & Trust Company, LLC, as the RLUSD issuer.

The OCC charter record shows that Ripple National Trust Bank’s charter became effective October 26. Ripple said Standard Custody would continue issuing RLUSD, with the separate national bank authorized to manage reserves and provide custody.

WisdomTree identifies a different New York trust, WisdomTree Digital Trust Company, LLC, as USDW’s issuer. For either coin, a federal waiver would have to cover issuance by the legal issuer.

ZUSD still appears on the Greenlist. Its issuer, GMO-Z.com Trust Company, Inc., removed the mint function in May during a voluntary wind-down. The issuer said direct redemptions ended November 11 and that remaining balances are subject to mandatory redemption. ZUSD’s wind-down was already underway months before Treasury published its proposal.

Under the GENIUS Act, January 18, 2027 is the effective date for the U.S. issuance restriction. If a state-regulated issuer has to pause, the immediate effect would be on its ability to supply fresh units to customers and distributors. Trading already-issued tokens and redeeming them with the issuer are separate activities.

Starting July 18, 2028, digital asset service providers such as exchanges generally can offer or sell payment stablecoins to people in the U.S. only if the issuer is permitted or an exception applies.

A separate foreign-issuer provision applies from the Act’s effective date. It conditions U.S. platform availability on the foreign issuer’s ability and commitment to comply with lawful orders and reciprocal arrangements. Section 18 also provides a route for foreign issuers from comparable regimes that register with the OCC and meet other conditions.

#5046. Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi has updated its online banking terms to make customers responsible for the actions of third-party AI agents accessing their accounts, including when an agent leaks their credentials and someone else uses them to get in.

The change, reported by TechCrunch Wednesday, makes explicit how the bank intends to handle an increasingly awkward question: Who pays when software handling your money makes a mistake?

Under Citi’s new terms, actions taken using credentials supplied to an outside agent are treated as authorized by the customer, even if the tool ignores instructions, exceeds its permissions, or shares those credentials with another party.

Citi says it has no obligation to investigate whether the agent followed the customer's original prompts, subject to applicable law.

A Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch that the November revision clarifies the risks of sharing banking credentials or granting outside tools account access. The provision does not apply to AI agents offered by Citi itself.

Citi is not acting alone. JPMorgan and Bank of America announced similar policies in October, while Citizens Bank warned customers this week by email that they could be responsible for what their AI assistants do.

PNC also changed its digital agreement on Wednesday. Its rules distinguish bank-approved connections, which can provide limited access, from handing an agent unrestricted access to an account.

The move follows a banking security scare from October 22. Major banks had detected AI agents logging into customer accounts, sometimes taking steps that appeared to go beyond their users' instructions, and executives said they struggled to determine which agents had been properly authorized.

An AI agent differs from a conventional chatbot because it can act on a user's behalf, potentially navigating websites, accessing financial records, or initiating payments. That makes a mistaken instruction or compromised login more consequential than a bad answer about a bank balance.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency has since issued AI examiner guidance that addresses outside agents, payment controls, and human approval. The framework is nonbinding and does not itself create new requirements for banks.

Citi's language does not automatically eliminate consumer fraud protections. Under U.S. federal consumer protection rules, banks generally must investigate unauthorized electronic transfers and limit customers' financial liability for fraud. Those protections cannot simply be waived through a banking agreement, although their application to transactions performed by customer-authorized AI agents remains less clear.

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau says banks cannot use agreements to waive those legal protections, although the rules distinguish theft by an outsider from transfers actually authorized by a customer or someone given access.

Whether a particular AI-related loss qualifies as unauthorized depends on how the transaction occurred.

PNC's updated agreement takes a stricter operational approach: Customers may use certain connection methods approved by the bank, but cannot give third-party AI agents their passwords or one-time security codes, and agents generally cannot initiate payments unless PNC expressly permits it.

It also urged customers to take care when trading CYBER, saying the security concern could lead to sharper price swings.

The Nov. 21–22 exchange notices affected three different ways customers could use CYBER.

Bithumb said CYBER deposits received during the pause would be credited in sequence after service resumed.

The lanes show service status based on the dated exchange notices. Line lengths do not represent the duration of a pause or delay.

#5037. CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

The UK's main crypto trade association has called on the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) to make sure that developers and governance participants in decentralized finance protocols are not treated as regulated firms by default.

CryptoUK set out its position in a response to the FCA's consultation paper CP26/36, which proposes guidance on the "perimeter" of the UK's new crypto regime: which activities fall inside it and need FCA authorization, and which don't. The group, which says it represents more than 150 crypto companies, submitted its response on Wednesday, November 18, the day the consultation closed, and published it a day later.

Yesterday, CryptoUK submitted its response to the Financial Conduct Authority’s Consultation Paper CP26/36, which proposes amendments to the Perimeter Guidance Manual (PERG) to reflect the FSMA 2000 (Cryptoassets) (Miscellaneous Amendments) Regulations 2026.

The consultation… pic.twitter.com/oOrZgAWscP

— CryptoUK (@CryptoUKAssoc) November 19, 2026

"Software development, maintenance, security work and participation in decentralised governance should not, by themselves, establish that a person carries on a regulated activity," CryptoUK wrote. That would be consistent with the FCA's "clear controlling person" approach to DeFi, under which regulation should hinge on who effectively controls a financial service, it argued.

CryptoUK said it "broadly" supports the draft guidance, which reflects further amendments to the UK's crypto legislation that the government laid in September. The FCA aims to publish its final guidance in January 2027. It welcomed new exclusions from the regime for temporary settlement and payment arrangements, assets backing UK-recognized stablecoins, proprietary trading and market making, and technical service providers.

But in several places, the draft "goes further than the legislation," the group said. It pointed to a proposed link to the UK based on where a protocol is developed or governed, requirements in the exclusions for payments and proprietary trading, and indicators that would narrow the exclusion for technical services. The FCA has acknowledged that the timing of the latest amendments created uncertainty for some firms, and said it will give applicants extra time where questions over the technical services exclusion held up their applications for authorization.

The group asked the FCA to publish a public record of stablecoins that qualify under the UK regime, to clarify how stablecoins differ from electronic money, and to include more examples of activities that typically fall outside the perimeter.

CryptoUK also urged the FCA to raise with the Treasury outcomes that put UK firms at a disadvantage, citing the treatment of UK technical providers that offer access to regulated overseas trading venues, and of payments using stablecoins issued abroad. The UK should align with the EU's MiCA framework "where appropriate," it said. The response was prepared with law firm CMS and signed by CryptoUK policy director Nick Brooks.

The consultation is part of the FCA's work to build out the UK's crypto rulebook. The regulator finalized its core rules for crypto firms in June, ahead of a regime due to take effect on October 25, 2027, and later issued guidance two weeks before its authorization window for crypto firms opened. It has also set out a shared vision for tokenization with the Bank of England.

#5034. VanEck to end Gemini ETF custody deals, keeps Coinbase

VanEck plans to end Gemini’s custody agreements for three crypto ETFs in February 2027, while Coinbase will continue serving as a custodian for the funds.

VanEck is cutting ties with Gemini as a custodian across three cryptocurrency exchange-traded funds (ETFs), ending a relationship that began with its first spot Bitcoin ETF.

VanEck notified Gemini on Monday that it would terminate custody and clearing agreements for its Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Solana (SOL) ETFs, according to separate filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) on Thursday.

The agreements will end “on or about” Feb. 16, 2027, while Coinbase Custody, which already serves the funds, will continue providing custody services.

The move raises questions about Gemini’s position in the US spot crypto ETF market, where Coinbase serves as custodian for many of the largest funds. The filings did not disclose the reasons for the terminations.

The termination extends beyond cryptocurrency custody. VanEck’s Solana ETF (VSOL) filing also covers a Gemini staking services agreement, raising questions about how the fund will handle staking after the transition.

Staking allows tokenholders to earn rewards by helping secure a proof-of-stake (PoS) blockchain such as Solana. For ETFs that stake their holdings, the service can provide an additional source of returns for the fund.

Cointelegraph contacted VanEck and Gemini for comment on the reason for terminating the agreements and the potential impact on the Solana ETF’s staking operations, but had not received a response by publication.

Gemini’s founders, Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss, were among the earliest proponents of spot Bitcoin ETFs in the United States, filing to launch the Winklevoss Bitcoin Trust in July 2013. US regulators did not approve the first spot Bitcoin ETFs until January 2024, more than a decade after the brothers’ initial filing.

Gemini became the original Bitcoin custodian for VanEck’s spot Bitcoin ETF when the fund launched in January 2024, before VanEck added Coinbase Custody in June that year.

Related: Gemini’s quarterly retail trading volume sinks to 3-year low

VanEck’s Bitcoin ETF (HODL) is considerably smaller than the largest US spot Bitcoin funds, holding about $880 million in assets under management, compared with $47.6 billion for BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) and $10.6 billion for Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC), according to Wallet Pilot data as of Thursday.

Gemini has also provided custody services for crypto ETFs in Canada and Australia. In the US, it continues to serve as a custodian for at least one smaller fund, the 7RCC Spot Bitcoin and Carbon Credit Futures ETF (BTCK), which identified Gemini and BitGo as its Bitcoin custodians in a September 2026 regulatory filing.

#5033. Bitcoin Price Slips Near $61,500 as Tech Selloff Spreads to Asia

The bitcoin price slipped to near $61,500 on Friday morning, according to Look Into Bitcoin data, as a selloff in technology stocks that followed Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut spread to Asian markets and dragged bitcoin to its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin first broke below $62,000 on Thursday evening, after Thursday afternoon’s report showed it giving back a rebound toward $64,000. The bitcoin price then slid to nearly $61,200 during Asian trading before bouncing back above $61,700. Friday marks a third straight day of losses.

The selling started in Asia. South Korea’s Kospi slid more than 5%, with chipmakers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics down around 6%. The Korea Exchange halted program selling for five minutes after the drop, while Japan’s Nikkei fell as much as 4.85% to a seven-month low.

U.S. futures pointed lower too. Nasdaq-100 futures fell more than 1.7% and S&P 500 futures dropped 0.8%, Yahoo Finance reported. Gold futures lost 3%. The S&P 500 is pointing to a seventh straight daily decline, its longest losing streak since the start of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020.

Anthropic shares fell 8% in premarket trading after closing their first session at $97.17, up 17% from the $83 IPO price. Nvidia was down about 2.4% and AMD fell nearly 7%, CNBC reported. Hawkish signals from Federal Reserve officials this week also weighed on sentiment, Reuters said.

Bitcoin funds are feeling the pressure. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $541.1 million in net outflows on Thursday, its largest single-day outflow since launch. That topped the previous record of $528.3 million set on Jan. 30. The fund bled $2 billion over nine straight days of outflows earlier this month.

Leveraged traders took heavy losses as the bitcoin price broke support. Bitcoin liquidations hit $712.9 million over 24 hours, with $614 million coming from long positions. Open interest in bitcoin futures fell to $18 billion from $20 billion a week ago, and funding rates flipped broadly negative as traders unwound their bets.

Traders are now watching whether the bitcoin price can hold the $60,000 area. A large pocket of long positions sits in the $60,500 to $60,900 band, just below the current price, and could come into play on a further drop. Bitcoin is also on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be its second-longest losing streak after nine weeks of declines in 2022.

Crypto stocks pointed lower in premarket trading. Strategy (MSTR) traded near $83.90, down about 2.6% from Thursday’s close of $86.13, after the stock fell for a sixth straight day on Thursday. Coinbase (COIN) slipped about 2.7% to near $106.

Bitcoin miners fell harder. IREN (IREN) dropped about 4.4%, CleanSpark (CLSK) lost about 3.6% and MARA Holdings (MARA) declined about 3.4%. Riot Platforms (RIOT) was down about 3.3%. IREN has also been under pressure as investors reassess AI infrastructure stocks. Delays in bringing some of its capacity online led Microsoft to spend less with the miner than planned last quarter.

Longer-term buyers have not left entirely. Institutional investors added 49,229 BTC through U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs in the third quarter, lifting their holdings to a record 356,491 BTC. Swiss bank Sygnum called the current downturn the shallowest bear market for bitcoin to date, putting the drawdown at around 54% compared with declines of more than 70% in previous cycles.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $61,800.

#5031. Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase is planning to launch its own prediction market contracts in the U.S. in the first half of 2027, as it looks to take more control of one of its fastest-growing businesses.

The exchange plans to offer the in-house event contracts to both retail and institutional customers, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing a person familiar with the matter.

A Coinbase spokesperson told Bloomberg the company had "nothing new to announce today," and pointed to plans it set out in October to expand its prediction markets and improve their economics by building its own clearing and other infrastructure. The spokesperson did not address the timing of an in-house launch.

Coinbase rolled out prediction markets across the U.S. through a partnership with Kalshi, after unveiling the product alongside stock trading last year. Offering its own contracts would give Coinbase more say over which markets it lists, and let it keep more of the revenue from trades.

The exchange has been laying the groundwork for its pivot. In December 2025, it agreed to buy prediction markets startup The Clearing Company, and in September 2026 it received approval from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) for its own clearinghouse. Coinbase already runs a regulated derivatives exchange.

Prediction markets have become a bright spot for Coinbase as crypto trading slows. The business passed $200 million in annualized revenue in the third quarter, with daily traders up more than 60% from the previous quarter, and revenue growing 82% a quarter on average since launch, the company reported in October.

"Prediction markets are adding another revenue stream and are less correlated to crypto volatility," CFO Alesia Haas said at the time. On an analyst call the same day, Haas said Coinbase's new clearinghouse meant it "can launch our own contracts, and we can also then earn better economics," while continuing to offer contracts through partners such as Kalshi. Asked when, she said: "We have not shared a timeline."

The products have faced legal challenges from states that argue some event contracts amount to unlicensed gambling. Coinbase sued three states over their attempts to regulate prediction markets, and New York sued Coinbase and Gemini over their offerings in April.

Last week, a Louisiana judge temporarily barred more than 30 prediction market providers, including Coinbase's derivatives arm, from offering event contracts in the state, after Attorney General Liz Murrill sued them on November 12, Forbes reported. The CFTC has sued Louisiana in federal court to block the order, and a hearing on a longer injunction is set for November 30.

Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, operates its own prediction market, Decrypt Predict.

#5028. Bitcoin Hovers Just Above Average Holder’s Cost Basis as Price Keeps Dipping

Bitcoin is trading just above its realized price, the average cost of every coin on the network, and a break below that level would put the average holder at a loss for the first time since early 2023, Glassnode, an onchain analytics firm posted Friday.

The price of bitcoin sat near $61,700 on Friday after sliding below $62,000. The realized price is a measure of what holders paid, not where the market trades. When bitcoin trades above it, the typical coin is sitting on a gain. Below it, the typical coin is underwater.

The margin is thin. The firm said the MVRV ratio, which compares bitcoin’s market value with its realized value, sits “just above 1.” A reading of 1 means the market price has fallen all the way to the realized price.

Realized price values each coin at the price it last moved on the blockchain, then averages across the supply. Look Into Bitcoin describes it as bitcoin’s average cost basis. Because it tracks the price when coins last changed hands, it moves far more slowly than the market.

The MVRV ratio expresses the same idea as a multiple. Above 1, holders are in profit on average. Below 1, they are not. A reading just above 1 means the market price is only a sliver higher than what the average coin cost.

The firm did not give the exact realized price in its post, which included a chart. That leaves the exact cushion between today’s price and the average cost basis unstated. Bitcoin Magazine Pro’s full realized price chart sits behind a login, so the current level could not be confirmed independently for this report.

“The average Bitcoin holder is still in profit,” the firm wrote. “The margin is thin.”

The slide has come with heavy selling from funds. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost about $541 million on Thursday, its largest one-day outflow since it launched in January 2024, Bloomberg reported. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total that day.

The selling stands out against the buying earlier in the fall. Institutions piled into bitcoin ETFs through the third quarter, pushing their combined holdings to a record, Bitcoin Magazine reported this week.

Leveraged traders took losses too. More than $700 million in leveraged crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, and about 86% of them were bets on higher prices. Bitcoin accounted for roughly $200 million of the total.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said the drop is part of a longer downturn rather than the start of a new one. “I see this as a continuation of the existing bear market rather than the start of another,” he said in a note dated Nov. 18.

The note frames Friday’s price action as a late stage of a decline that began well before November. Butterfill pointed to whale selling that weighed on prices from late 2025, expectations of higher interest rates and weakness in technology stocks, all of which have kept buyers cautious.

“Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said. “Geopolitics will have a considerable say in that.”

A move through the realized price would put bitcoin near the bottom edge of that range. The firm said holders as a group have not been in loss since early 2023.

A sustained breakout, Butterfill said, would likely require “a change in the policy outlook, renewed whale participation or both.”

#5024. Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin slid below $62,000 on Friday, extending a weeklong sell-off as investors pulled money from spot Bitcoin ETFs and braced for another interest rate hike from the Federal Reserve.

The leading cryptocurrency was trading at around $61,780, down 2.8% over the past day and 10% over the past week, after touching a 24-hour low of $61,273—its lowest level since early July—according to CoinGecko data. It has fallen more than $2,000 since struggling to hold $64,000 earlier this week, and has also formed a so-called death cross, a bearish chart pattern.

The drop triggered $727 million in liquidations across crypto markets over the past 24 hours, about 86% of which were long positions betting on higher prices, according to CoinGlass. Bitcoin accounted for just under $202 million of the total, with more than 151,000 traders liquidated.

Spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $582.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, their largest single-day outflow since October 30, according to SoSoValue data. The funds have lost more than $1.1 billion so far this week and almost $3.5 billion in November, while their total net assets have fallen to $76.6 billion.

"Within crypto, the most telling sign is this month's outflow from spot Bitcoin ETFs, which suggests investors are choosing to stay on the sidelines for now," Neil Steinhardt, head of Nexo U.S., told Decrypt.

Treasury yields above 5% and expectations that the Fed will raise rates in December have weighed on assets that don't pay interest, including Bitcoin and gold, Steinhardt said. "Because the pressure appears tied to the rate backdrop, and that backdrop has shifted before, a turn in yields or the dollar would likely be the first signal that appetite is returning," he added, pointing to the December 4 jobs report and the Fed's December 9 meeting as the next checkpoints.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller has called for a larger, half-point increase at that meeting, unless policymakers signal another rise in January. Traders are betting on a smaller move: CME FedWatch puts the odds of a quarter-point hike on December 9 at 99.2%, with the chance of a half-point increase falling to almost zero from more than 9% on Thursday morning.

The decline has left the average Bitcoin holder only just in profit. Bitcoin is still trading above its realized price, the average price at which every coin on the network last moved, but the margin is thin, onchain analytics firm Glassnode tweeted on Friday. A break below would put holders in loss for the first time since early 2023, it said.

#5023. Block Says Most Bitcoin Payments on Square Are for Coffee and Food

Most bitcoin payments at Square sellers are small, everyday purchases at coffee shops and restaurants, Block said in a new report that it hopes will inform a debate in Congress over a tax break for small crypto purchases.

The report, “The State of Bitcoin Payments,” covers completed bitcoin payments at U.S. Square sellers from Nov. 1, 2025, to Oct. 15, 2026. The median payment was about $13, and the average was about $29.

Food and drink businesses took 72% of those payments, Block found. That is a bigger share than the category’s 67.5% slice of regular card payments on Square over the same period.

“Most bitcoin payments are small and routine,” the company wrote on X Thursday, calling the report one of the first datasets on how people spend bitcoin in the local economy.

Coffee shops and fast food businesses accounted for 62.6% of the bitcoin payments in the food and drink category, with a median payment of $9.10. Grocery stores made up a smaller share but had the highest average payment in the group, at $60.73.

Small tickets dominated. About 42% of bitcoin payments on Square were under $10, and 65.1% were under $20. Those sub-$20 payments made up just 19.1% of total payment value, though, with a few larger purchases accounting for much of the dollars spent.

Customers also came back. Among Cash App users who paid with bitcoin at least twice at the same merchant, the median was three purchases per customer-merchant pair. About 44% of bitcoin payments from Cash App were repeat payments, and 86% of those came within 30 days.

The activity was not limited to Block’s own app. External Lightning wallets such as Strike, Wallet of Satoshi, Phoenix and Coinbase handled 56.6% of Square bitcoin payments and about 66% of payment value, while Cash App wallets made up the rest.

Square sellers can choose how much of each sale they keep in bitcoin, and Square converts the rest to dollars automatically. Block said zero-fee processing for bitcoin payments on Square has been extended through Dec. 31, 2027. Local governments are testing similar setups, with Miami-Dade County now taking bitcoin for property taxes and converting it to dollars.

Block tied the data to tax policy. Under current U.S. federal rules, paying with bitcoin generally means calculating a capital gain or loss on every purchase, even a $10 coffee, the company said.

A Morning Consult survey that Block commissioned found that 23.6% of businesses that don’t accept bitcoin cite tax and accounting complexity as a reason. That is about the same share that cite regulation, refunds or price swings. Among businesses that have considered taking bitcoin but haven’t, the figure was 27%.

Consumers pointed to friction, too. Morning Consult surveyed 8,669 U.S. adults who are aware of bitcoin in August. Among those who don’t own it, 29.6% said they would be more likely to pay with bitcoin if it were easier, and 15.7% of that group said they were unsure how it is taxed or regulated.

Block said Congress is weighing a crypto tax package and pointed to a de minimis exemption, which would exempt small gains on qualifying purchases, as one option. Industry groups have pressed lawmakers on crypto tax rules for months, with little movement so far.

The report doesn’t give totals. Block said the number of Square bitcoin payments, the number of sellers receiving them and total bitcoin payment volume are proprietary and not disclosed. Merchant lists like the 425 Los Angeles businesses that accept bitcoin offer a rough sense of reach.

“We think tax policy should account for how people spend bitcoin as well as how they hold it,” Block said.

#5021. Binance hacker moves nearly $600M in ETH, routes funds through THORChain, Chainflip

OnChain Lens tracked nearly $600 million in ETH transfers over two days as the Binance hackers moved the stolen funds through fresh wallets and cross-chain protocols to obscure their trail.

Wallets linked to the Binance hack moved nearly $600 million in Ether over two days, with some of the stolen funds routed through cross-chain swap protocols, according to blockchain analysts.

The attackers moved about $594 million in Ether (ETH) to fresh wallets, $248 million on Thursday and another 197,460 ETH, worth approximately $346 million, on Friday. Attackers routed part of the funds through cross-chain protocols THORChain and Chainflip, according to blockchain data provider Onchain Lens.

About $5 million was routed through Chainflip, while THORChain accounted for most of the cross-chain activity tracked by blockchain analyst SomaXBT, according to estimates he shared in a Friday X response. Onchain analyst Specter also estimated that only a “small portion” of the funds moved through Chainflip, which has said it screens deposits for illicit funds, although it previously acknowledged gaps in those checks.

North Korean hackers have previously used THORChain to launder funds stolen from some of the largest crypto hacks, including the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in February 2025 and the $388 million Bitget hack in September 2026. TRM Labs and Chainalysis have linked the $6.3 billion Binance hack to North Korea-linked hacking group TraderTraitor.

Following the latest transfers, the Binance hackers’ publicly tracked addresses held approximately 224,722 ETH, worth about $394 million, according to blockchain data platform Arkham.

THORChain’s 24-hour swap volume soared to $239 million on Friday, pushing the protocol’s earnings to $243,000, according to THORChain’s dashboard.

#5020. Michael Saylor sees up to $7.5 billion in ‘natural demand’ for Strategy’s STRC preferred stock

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Saylor said the credit-market contraction began around May, and the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level.

Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said "natural, organic" demand for the company's STRC preferred stock at its current 12% dividend rate is likely between $5 billion and $7.5 billion.

"I don't know exactly where the number is, but I’m going to go out on a limb and say that somewhere between $5 billion and $7.5 billion, there is natural organic demand to hold the instrument at 12% dividend," Saylor said during a live investor Q&A on Thursday.

His comments come as Strategy has been actively repurchasing STRC shares at prices below its $100 stated level. President and CEO Phong Le said the company has bought back about $3 billion of the preferred stock at prices between $95 and $99, below the issuance price level.

Saylor described the current environment as a credit-market contraction that began around May. He said the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level that the market will support at the existing dividend rate.

"We're going to contract the outstanding STRC because 12% is a fat dividend for an investment-grade instrument,” Saylor said. "As we buy it back, we lengthen the duration, we strip the risk, we improve the credit."

CEO Le also said that the recent buybacks have de-risked the company's balance sheet after credit markets tightened over the past four to five months. Le added that Strategy had previously expanded STRC from an initial $2.5 billion issuance to roughly $8 billion under favorable market conditions.

Saylor framed the support for STRC as important for the long-term credibility of the product. He compared it to an insurance company paying claims, arguing that failing to back the security in a weaker market would damage confidence among credit investors over multiple years.

"When the company is swapping either BTC or MSTR for STRC, we're supporting the credit, we're supporting the brand of the company, we're improving the product quality," Saylor said.

The Strategy Chairman also reiterated that the company has at least three years' worth of cash right now, so it will still be able to operate even if the current bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) downturn continues.

The world's largest digital asset treasury firm owned 850,849 BTC as of Nov. 15, according to its latest disclosure, which amounts to more than 4% of bitcoin's maximum supply cap. Last month, holders of Strategy's STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shares approved a change allowing dividends to accrue daily, rather than on the previous schedule.

Last week, the company sold approximately $244.7 million in MSTR shares to fund bitcoin purchases and STRC repurchases.

#5017. Bitcoin Price Crashes to $59,000 as Market Selloff Deepens

The bitcoin price crashed below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since early July, falling about $1,800 in roughly an hour as a deepening U.S. tech stock selloff dragged risk assets lower.

Bitcoin traded near $61,400 at 9:40 a.m. ET and slid to about $59,600 by 10:45 a.m., a drop of nearly 3%, according to Look Into Bitcoin data. The bitcoin price is down about $3,400, or more than 5%, from the same time Thursday, when it traded near $63,000.

The break of $60,000 is a key psychological level, and it came with little warning. Bitcoin price had held above $61,000 for most of the morning, even after a third straight daily loss.

Wall Street opened sharply lower. The Nasdaq Composite fell more than 2% in morning trading as shares of Anthropic extended losses in its second day since a record Nasdaq debut. Nvidia and AMD also traded lower.

Bitcoin has been moving with tech stocks. The market has questioned whether heavy spending on artificial intelligence will pay off, and Friday’s open did nothing to ease that worry.

Rate expectations are adding pressure. Traders see a roughly 94% chance the Federal Reserve raises rates in December, CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed. Higher rates tend to hurt bitcoin and other assets that pay no yield.

Earlier Friday, South Korea’s Kospi index fell more than 5% and chipmakers sank across Asia. That selling carried into U.S. trading and then into bitcoin.

Some analysts had flagged $60,000 as a floor. Katie Stockton of Fairlead Strategies told CNBC the bottom of bitcoin’s trading range sits “just below 60,000,” and that it “appears to be trying to find a footing.” Friday’s drop tests that view.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said this week the drop is a “continuation of the existing bear market.” His base case is range trading around $60,000 to $90,000 through much of 2027.

JUST IN: Bitcoin falls below $60,000 for the first time since June.BTC is now less than 4% away from a new cycle low 👀 pic.twitter.com/GSZkbGdzZz

Funds have been sellers. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost $541.1 million on Thursday, its biggest one-day outflow on record. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total, extending a streak of heavy withdrawals this week.

Leveraged traders were hit as well. More than $700 million in crypto positions were liquidated in the 24 hours before the latest drop, and about 86% were bets on higher prices. The slide below $60,000 likely forced out more of them.

The move extends a month-long slump. Bitcoin has dropped roughly $20,000 in the past month, a slide tracked as the price fell under $62,000 on Thursday.

Crypto stocks fell even faster. Strategy (MSTR) was down about 7% near $79.85, a day after it tumbled for a sixth straight session. Coinbase (COIN) fell about 3% to $105.36. Shares of Strive (ASST) fell to $15.31 Friday morning, down 21.47%, or $4.19, over the past five days. The stock has slid steadily from near $19.50 as bitcoin price’s sell-off deepened, with its sharpest drop in Friday’s trading.

Bitcoin miners took the worst of it. MARA Holdings (MARA) dropped about 11% to $5.55. Riot Platforms (RIOT) lost about 11% to $15.85, and CleanSpark (CLSK) fell about 12% to $5.13.

Data center operators that also mine bitcoin were hit hard. IREN fell about 12% to $12.62, and Cipher Digital (CIFR) dropped 14% to $5.58. Both have traded with AI stocks, which are also under pressure.

The bitcoin price is now at its lowest since the first days of July. Bitcoin set its record above $126,000 in October 2025, so it has lost more than half its value from that peak.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,600.
