#5016. Senate passes ‘Death Bets Act’ to ban prediction market contracts tied to death and war

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Death Bets Act, which now heads to the House, would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The U.S. Senate has passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The S.4035 bill, titled "Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy, and Harm Betting in Event Trading Systems Act," cleared the Senate on Thursday and now heads to the House of Representatives.

Sen. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) introduced the bill in March 2026 as primary sponsor, with Sen. John Curtis (R-Utah) as a co-sponsor. The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA) to prohibit listing contracts related to war, death, and similar activities.

"[These contracts] create rampant opportunities for insider trading, and perhaps worst of all, they're offered with no regard for national security and, in far too many cases, even basic morality," Schiff said Thursday.

Schiff added that prediction markets are operating in a "Wild West" and are "desperately in need of regulation and guardrails."

Under the CEA, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission has the authority to block contracts tied to war, terrorism, or assassination. While the final decision is at the agency's discretion, the lawmakers backing the Death Bets Act argued that such flexibility is no longer sufficient.

Prediction market platforms have also taken steps to remove controversial contracts. Earlier this year, Polymarket took down a market that let users bet on the likelihood of a nuclear weapon detonation due to criticism on social media.

The congressional push adds to the mounting legal pressure prediction market platforms are already facing. More than a dozen states have taken legal or enforcement actions against Kalshi for allegedly violating state gambling laws.

Meanwhile, federal regulators and state authorities remained locked in escalating legal battles over who has the authority to regulate prediction market platforms. The CFTC has maintained that it has jurisdiction over prediction markets under the CEA, while several states have argued that certain event contracts violate state gambling laws.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said during a Thursday interview at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum that the agency would work with the Justice Department to defend its jurisdiction in legal disputes.

In recent weeks, the CFTC and the DOJ have filed lawsuits against several states, including Utah, Arkansas, and Louisiana, seeking to block state enforcement against prediction market platforms.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.

#5015. MoonPay Applies for National Trust Bank Charter to Grow Custody and Stablecoin Business

MoonPay Applies for National Trust Bank Charter to Grow Custody and Stablecoin Business

Crypto payments firm MoonPay has applied for a U.S. national bank charter, joining a growing list of crypto companies seeking a federal banking license.

The proposed MoonPay National Trust Bank would be based in Miami Beach, Florida, according to the company's November 17 application to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), Bloomberg reported on Thursday. The charter would help MoonPay grow its custody business and expand its stablecoin services, according to the application.

MoonPay confirmed the move on Thursday, tweeting that it was "proud to share" that it had applied for a U.S. national bank charter with the OCC.

today we are proud to share that MoonPay has applied for a US National Bank Charter with the OCC 🇺🇸 pic.twitter.com/XGHBPx84v0

Unlike a full-service bank, a national trust bank can hold and manage assets for clients, but can't take deposits or lend money. The OCC did not immediately respond to Bloomberg's request for comment.

Founded in 2019, MoonPay is best known for providing on- and off-ramps between fiat currencies and crypto. It has been expanding into new areas including AI, having launched a crypto wallet designed for AI chatbots, let users buy crypto through ChatGPT, and acquired AI trading copilot startup Dawn Labs.

It has also been courting institutions. In April, it acquired Sodot, a Tel Aviv-based crypto key management firm, to form the basis of MoonPay Institutional, a unit serving banks, asset managers and trading firms. The unit is led by former acting Commodity Futures Trading Commission chair Caroline Pham.

MoonPay is the latest crypto firm to seek a federal charter under President Donald Trump's administration. Earlier this month, Coinbase joined Circle and Ripple in receiving final OCC approval for a national trust bank, after Ripple won approval for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin. Jack Dorsey's Block has also applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin, while World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture tied to the Trump family, won conditional approval for its own trust company in August.

The approvals have drawn pushback. A banking group has sued over Block's application, accusing crypto firms of seeking "side door" access to banking, while Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's approvals of Coinbase and Ripple were illegal, a claim the crypto industry has disputed.

Warren is expected to chair the Senate Banking Committee from January, and is expected to examine World Liberty's charter as part of a wider look at Trump-linked crypto ventures.

#5012. MoonPay applies for national trust bank charter to expand stablecoin services

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

MoonPay plans to offer custody and stablecoin services through the proposed bank, subject to OCC approval.

Crypto payments firm MoonPay announced Thursday it had filed an application with the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to launch MoonPay National Trust Bank.

The application, dated Nov. 17 and received by the OCC on Nov. 18, said MoonPay's trust bank would provide fiduciary custody services; stablecoin services, including issuance, redemption, and reserve management; and conversion services as an agent.

The filing is to organize MoonPay National Trust Bank as a non-depository national bank, limited to trust company operations and related activities. It would not seek federal deposit insurance and would operate as a member bank of the Federal Reserve.

If approved, the national charter would provide a single federal framework for MoonPay to offer custody and stablecoin-related services. MoonPay’s application comes ahead of the GENIUS Act taking effect on Jan. 18, 2027, when payment stablecoin issuers will generally need approval under the new framework.

Several other major crypto firms have received full or conditional approval for the same type of OCC national trust bank charter.

Last December, the OCC granted conditional approvals to Circle (CRCL), Ripple, BitGo, Fidelity Digital Assets, and Paxos. Circle received final approval in July 2026, while Ripple received its full approval in October. Coinbase's (COIN) became effective earlier this month. Additional firms, including Stripe's Bridge, Crypto.com, and World Liberty Financial, hold conditional approvals.

Meanwhile, MoonPay has expanded its regulated and enterprise footprint over the past year. It received its New York BitLicense in June 2025 and its New York trust charter in November 2025.

Earlier this year, MoonPay agreed to acquire private markets platform North Capital in an all-stock deal. It launched enterprise stablecoin services in November 2025 and partnered with WisdomTree to incorporate a tokenized Treasury fund into stablecoin reserves. It has also announced a strategic collaboration with Dunamu, the parent company of South Korean crypto exchange Upbit, to explore stablecoin-based global payments and fund movement.

Former Commodity Futures Trading Commission Acting Chair Caroline Pham also joined MoonPay as chief legal officer and chief administrative officer last December.

#5011. Lagarde warns Europe could miss chance to unify tokenized markets

The ECB president said Europe’s market for tokenized securities could split into platforms that cannot settle trades with one another.

European Central Bank (ECB) President Christine Lagarde warned Friday that Europe needs common rules for tokenized securities before incompatible standards take hold across trading platforms.

Speaking at the Frankfurt European Banking Congress, Lagarde said blockchain technology lets firms issue, trade and settle securities on a single platform. The global markets for tokenized assets was worth about 23 billion euros in March, five times its size a year earlier, she said.

Europe could build a single market for those assets, Lagarde said, but if platforms adopt different rules as they grow, they may be unable to settle trades with one another. She said lawmakers have a role alongside the ECB.

“Common rules will be needed too, and those lie with legislators,” she said.

The Eurosystem launched Pontes in September, giving financial institutions a way to settle tokenized securities trades in central bank money. Earlier in November, the European Stability Mechanism used Pontes to settle a 1 billion euro (about $1.1 million) digital bond.

Through a second project, Appia, the Eurosystem is working with market participants on common standards for tokenized securities. It plans to publish a blueprint in 2028.

#5010. U.S. Treasury Secretary Pushes Back on Bigger Fed Hike

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent pushed back Friday on calls for a bigger Federal Reserve rate hike, even as bitcoin broke below $60,000 for the first time since July and stocks sank on bets that the Fed will keep tightening into a selloff.

Bessent told CNBC he would encourage the Fed not to embrace a half-point increase at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. He cited the energy supply shock and a drop in crude prices as reasons for the central bank to hold back.

He argued that one governor now backing a 50 basis point hike had wanted a similar cut within the last year. Bessent said it “seems somewhat peripatetic” to want the bigger increase only 11 or 12 months later. He did not name the governor.

“I would encourage the Fed to look forward, not backwards,” Bessent said.

The comments came two days after Fed Governor Christopher Waller told CNBC a half-point hike is probably necessary in December and that the central bank may need to “shock people.” Waller said inflation is “headed in the wrong direction, and it’s spreading out.”

Waller is not alone. Minutes of the October meeting showed nearly all officials expect a quarter-point hike in December, and two floated a larger one. Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee also declined to rule out a bigger move on Thursday.

“We’ve now had multiple months in a row of borderline disturbingly high inflation rates with little progress in the sectors where we would expect to be seeing some progress,” Goolsbee told reporters. “So everything’s always on the table.”

Not everyone wants to go bigger. New York Fed President John Williams said Tuesday that “I don’t think the situation calls for dramatic action.” St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem has suggested as many as four hikes by the spring of 2027.

The Fed raised rates in September for the first time in more than three years and held them steady in October. Inflation has stayed hot since the Iran strikes pushed up energy prices earlier this year.

Citi still expects a quarter-point increase in December. Global chief economist Nathan Sheets said Thursday he does not see a need for 50 basis points despite persistent inflation. In a note Friday, the bank wrote that “Fed officials will consequently have little appetite to hike into an equity sell-off.”

Citi expects the Fed to hold after December and begin cutting later in 2027. Traders agree on the next step: the CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed a more than 98% chance of a quarter-point hike, and the 10-year Treasury yield fell more than 10 basis points to 4.95% as investors bought bonds.

Stocks fell for another day. The Nasdaq Composite dropped more than 2% in morning trading, and the VIX volatility index topped 30, its highest level since March.

Bitcoin traded near $59,700 shortly before 11 a.m. ET, down more than 5% on the day, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro. It broke below $60,000 for the first time since early July and is on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, the longest streak since 2022.

Higher rates are a headwind for assets that pay no yield, and the selloff has reached companies that hold bitcoin. Strategy’s stock fell for a sixth straight session on Thursday, and the bitcoin price has dropped roughly 25% in a month, a slide Bitcoin Magazine tracked earlier this week.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill tied the outlook to inflation in a note dated Nov. 18. “Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,300.

Ark Invest trimmed its Ethereum and Solana staking ETF exposure while buying nearly 1 million shares in Anthropic on Thursday.

Cathie Wood-led Ark Invest bought 934,968 Anthropic shares, worth $90.9 million at Thursday's closing price, across six of its exchange-traded funds on its Nasdaq debut while offloading some of its Ethereum and Solana staking ETF holdings.

Some 60% of Ark's Anthropic buys were for its ARKK Innovation ETF, though they only represent around 0.4% of that fund's total holdings, according to the firm's latest disclosures, which are topped by $2.3 billion worth of Tesla shares.

More specifically, it sold $2.9 million worth of its 3iQ Ethereum staking ETF holdings from its ARKW Next Generation Technology fund and ARKF Blockchain & Fintech Innovation. It also offloaded $1.2 million in 3iQ Solana ETF shares from the same funds.

Ark's investment strategy aims to let no individual holding take up more than 10% of a fund's portfolio to maintain diversification. Ark is likely to continue rebalancing its fund weightings if the value of certain shares rise or fall significantly compared to other holdings.

Anthropic's (ANT) shares opened at $111.50 in its Nasdaq debut on Thursday, 34% above the $83 initial public offering price, pushing its market value as high as $2.1 trillion to top SpaceX's June listing. ANT slipped back later in the trading day, but closed Thursday up 17.1% from the IPO price at $97.17, per TradingView.

Anthropic had priced 980 million shares at $83 each, above its original $70 to $78 range and more than the 825 million shares it had planned to sell, for a total offering value of about $81.3 billion. That could rise to about $93.5 billion if underwriters fully exercise their option to buy another 147 million shares. A planned $26 billion private placement with AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC would bring the combined total to about $107.3 billion before any exercise of the underwriters' option.

Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, told The Block the IPO was "historic," citing its record raise and Anthropic's first-day gain. However, he also said the stock's retreat from its intraday high "could be considered slightly bearish" as the broader market sold off.

Anthropic is the first frontier AI company to go public, with OpenAI also eyeing a listing in 2027.

Meanwhile, the Toronto Stock Exchange-listed 3iQ Ethereum staking ETF closed up 0.4% on Thursday at $6.85, with its 3iQ Solana ETF counterpart trading down 0.3% for the day, per TradingView. The underlying cryptocurrencies, ETH Ethereum (ETH) and SOL Solana (SOL), fell 1.1% and 0.7%, respectively, on Thursday, according to The Block's prices page.

#5007. Morning Minute: Anthropic IPO Pops, Sinks Markets

Morning Minute: Anthropic IPO Pops, Sinks Markets

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

The largest base IPO deal ever priced at $83 a share Wednesday night, opened Thursday morning at $111.50, and briefly pushed Anthropic’s fully diluted valuation past $2 trillion. The stock finished at $97.17, up 17% from the offer and down 13% from where it opened (it has since fallen about 15% to $88).

A 17% first-day gain on an $81.34 billion raise is a successful listing by any normal standard.

The rest of the market did not fare as well.

The Nasdaq fell 2.06%. The S&P 500 dropped 1.9%. The Dow lost 2.17% and closed below 50,000 for the first time since June. Tech had rallied before the bell on the listing and reversed by the afternoon.

MD fell 7.73%. Nebius dropped 7.8%. CoreWeave lost 5%. Nvidia slid 3.4% despite beating earnings last week. Oracle, Micron, Broadcom and Microsoft all finished lower. The biggest AI listing in history happened, and AI infrastructure was the worst place to be holding.

Joe Maher at Capital Economics put it plainly: “Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO has failed to rekindle enthusiasm for tech stocks.”

Crypto went with it. Bitcoin fell to $61,500, its lowest since early July. That’s a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be the longest run since the nine-week stretch in 2022. Eth hit the $1,750 level, down 28% in thirty days. Gold fell to a fourteen-month low. Silver dropped 3%.

When Bitcoin, equities and gold all sell off on the same session, the common factor is usually the discount rate.

That’s the Fed. Chris Waller spent this week making the case for fifty basis points in December, and the minutes showed he isn’t alone. FxPro’s Alex Kuptsikevich described the market as spooked by “the spectre of a double rate hike” and pointed out Bitcoin is now more than 7% below its 200-week moving average, the deepest it has traded below that line in three years.

So where does this leave us heading into next week?

Start with what an $81 billion IPO actually does to a market. That money came from somewhere. Add the separate $26 billion private placement from Nvidia, Google, AMD and four other suppliers, and roughly $107 billion of capital got committed to a single name in one day. Kuptsikevich raised the possibility that the listing absorbed the riskiest money in the system, and that if so, some of it rotates back out once the allocation settles.

That’s the bull case for crypto over the next few weeks. It requires the Fed to cooperate.

For the bear case, one can simply look at SpaceX.

SpaceX priced at $135 in June, opened at $150, and closed its first day up 19%. Then it kept running, hitting $225.64 four days later, up 67% from the offer. The unwind started the following session. By June 22 it had fallen 16% in a single day and sat 31% off the high. By mid-July it traded below its IPO price. By late July it was down roughly 50% from the peak.

That would project out to a market low around Christmas time, which is actually how the 2018 midterm cycle played out. Will history repeat itself? We will find out soon enough. In the interim, expect volatility.

#5003. Researchers Publish Security Analysis of Bitcoin’s Post-Quantum SHRINCS Proposal

Several researchers have published a 131-page security analysis of SHRINCS, a proposed post-quantum signature scheme for Bitcoin, and are asking the wider community to look for flaws in it. Researcher Mikhail Kudinov announced the first version Thursday in a thread on X, saying the paper reduces SHRINCS’ security to the properties of its underlying hash function.

The analysis, dated Nov. 18, estimates classical security of 121.4 to 122.7 bits for SHRINCS, though those figures rest on assumptions that include three unproven conjectures. Kudinov said the work is not finished. The team is still working on a new analysis of a property called SM-DSPR, whose bound has so far been conjectured, and on tightening a reprogramming step and improving a bound against quantum attackers.

SHRINCS is a hash-based scheme, meaning its security rests on hash functions rather than the elliptic curve math behind today’s Bitcoin signatures. It places two designs under one key: a stateful part built on FXMSS with WOTS+C, and a stateless fallback built on SLH-DSA. Because both use the same hash function, Kudinov said, the proof has to cover both.

Signature size is the main cost. Under the draft proposal, stateful SHRINCS signatures run from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while the stateless fallback is 5,777 bytes. Schnorr signatures on Bitcoin today are 64 bytes, and ECDSA signatures typically come in around 71. Signatures take up scarce block space, so larger ones mean fewer transactions per block.

The compact stateful path carries a danger. It relies on one-time keys, and if a wallet accidentally reuses one, an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal the funds. Wallet software therefore has to track the state of every key. The stateless path avoids the problem, but at a much larger size.

Jonas Nick, Blockstream’s director of research, told Isabel Foxen Duke, head of the MARA Foundation, on MARA Foundation TV that wallets must automatically fall back to the stateless path whenever a state counter could be compromised. “It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” he said. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

Nick also warned that heavy use of the larger signatures would weigh on the network. “We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

The authors are clear that the analysis needs scrutiny. Kudinov said it has not been sufficiently peer reviewed, and the project’s GitHub repository warns that the documents may contain errors and should not be the sole basis for deploying SHRINCS. “We tried to be careful, but a proof of this size can have mistakes,” he wrote, asking readers to open an issue if they find one.

The team also produced two results that are not specific to SHRINCS: a classical bound for a property called ITSR without reprogramming, and a quadratic quantum bound for undetectability. Kudinov said they also apply to SPHINCS+ and SLH-DSA, the hash-based schemes SHRINCS borrows from, and that they were published as separate papers.

The SHRINCS work lands as quantum risk climbs the agenda. A global securities regulators’ group this week warned that moving blockchains to quantum-resistant cryptography may be especially hard, and researchers recently built a post-quantum defense for the Lightning Network after Bitcoin’s first quantum-safe transaction was shown without a fork.

The draft Bitcoin Improvement Proposal for SHRINCS is also open for comment. “We need all the possible feedback — engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said of it. “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise.”

#5002. Senate Passes Bill Banning Prediction Market Bets on War, Terrorism and Death

Senate Passes Bill Banning Prediction Market Bets on War, Terrorism and Death

The Senate has passed a bill that would ban prediction markets from offering bets on war, terrorism, assassinations and deaths, sending the measure to the House of Representatives.

The Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy, and Harm Betting in Event Trading Systems Act, or DEATH BETS Act, passed the Senate by voice vote on Wednesday, November 19, with an amendment, according to Congress.gov. The bill was introduced by Sen. Adam Schiff (D-CA) in March, and is cosponsored by Sen. John Curtis (R-UT).

"With this bill, Congress is making it clear that these bets should be illegal," Schiff tweeted after the vote.

BREAKING: The Senate just passed my bill with @SenJohnCurtis to prohibit prediction markets from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war, military operations, or an individual’s death.

With this bill, Congress is making it clear that these bets should be… pic.twitter.com/Mzu5PWmGsL

— Senator Adam Schiff (@SenAdamSchiff) November 20, 2026

The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act to bar exchanges and clearinghouses registered with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) from listing or clearing event contracts that expressly reference terrorism, assassination, war or U.S. military operations.

It would also ban contracts that, at the time of listing, are "reasonably expected to resolve principally based on a death of an individual." The CFTC would be able to extend the ban to "any similar activity" by rule.

The bill would not affect the status of other types of contracts covered by existing CFTC rules on event contracts, such as those involving gaming, according to its text.

Prediction markets such as Kalshi and Polymarket let users trade on the outcome of real-world events, from elections and sports to geopolitics. Markets on wars and the fates of world leaders have drawn criticism from lawmakers, who have argued they let people profit from tragedy, and could reward insiders with knowledge of military plans. Polymarket users have been accused of insider trading after making $2.4 million on Iran-related predictions. Polymarket pulled a market on whether a nuclear weapon would be detonated after a public backlash.

Senate Democrats have raised concerns that people close to President Donald Trump could profit from war-related markets, and one senator has alleged White House corruption over Iran war predictions. Kalshi was also sued after refusing to pay out on a market tied to the death of Iran's then-Supreme Leader, Ali Khamenei.

The CFTC has also moved to curb such markets. In June, it proposed rules that would ban markets on outcomes that could come about through war or assassination, such as vaguely worded bets on when U.S. adversaries would be "out of office," which Kalshi and Polymarket had used to sidestep existing bans on war-related contracts.

War-related markets have also appeared elsewhere, including on Myriad, the prediction market operated by Decrypt's parent company DASTAN, where, where users traded on whether U.S. troops would enter Iran.

The bill's passage comes as prediction markets face growing legal pressure from states, which argue that some event contracts amount to unlicensed gambling. New York, for example, has sued Coinbase and Gemini over their prediction markets. The CFTC, which first sued three states in April to defend its authority over the markets, has now taken 12 states to court, most recently Louisiana.

#5000. Wintermute policy chief calls for crypto lobby to ‘clean house’ in D.C.

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The call comes as banks and lawmakers push back against the SEC's tokenization exemption.

Wintermute's head of policy and advocacy, Ron Hammond, urged the crypto industry to "clean" its house in Washington following what he called a series of lobbying setbacks amid policymaking.

Speaking at the Capital Crypto Forum on Thursday, Hammond said the industry has struggled to counter larger financial interests as tokenization brings crypto and traditional finance into direct competition.

"I would say we probably need to clean house a little bit on the policy side here," Hammond said. "There's been fractures in the industry that show that either A, we're not united enough, or B, when we go up against a larger force, we just don't win”

His comments come a month after the Securities and Exchange Commission in September introduced a five-year innovation exemption that opened a route for trading tokenized U.S. stocks.

However, the policy has drawn resistance from both traditional finance companies and lawmakers, with Hammud also having some reservations.

"We see it being really limited and tailored in its final form," he said. " But effectively, even for companies like us, it doesn't really do much good for us. I mean, the limits are pretty strong. So that's why we're saying it's a great first step, but we have a lot more ways to go here."

Dozens of major companies, including JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs, Wells Fargo, and McDonald's, have objected to third-party tokenized versions of their shares under the SEC's exemption process.

"It's weird to see in New York, we are partnering with a lot of these firms that I'm lobbying against here in D.C. [...]," Hammond said. "We partner with the banks all the time, but in D.C. we are against them on a lot of fronts."

Hammond also criticized crypto's choice of political allies, saying some lawmakers who campaigned as pro-crypto later opposed tokenization.

Earlier this week, four Democratic senators, including Angela Alsobrooks, Ruben Gallego, Mark Warner and Catherine Cortez Masto, warned SEC Chair Paul Atkins that the exemption left gaps around know-your-customer and anti-money-laundering requirements.

The SEC's tokenization exemption is also facing questions from within its incoming leadership. On Thursday, Kristin Johnson, Trump's Democratic pick for the commission, said at a Senate Banking Committee hearing that she would welcome a formal rulemaking process with public comment, given concerns raised by those opposing the rule.

Meanwhile, the industry also faces competition from other jurisdictions, Hammond said, as officials seek to attract digital-asset businesses.

"When I go over to the UK, they love to hear any moment that America has a gap. Like when Clarity failed, they were all asking me, 'What can we do to get these firms back to the UK?'"

#4997. Rep. Horsford sees opening for House vote on crypto tax bill before Congress ends

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Digital Asset Tax Certainty Act, introduced by Rep. Jason Smith, passed the House Ways and Means Committee in September and includes most provisions from the PARITY Act, Horsford said.

Rep. Steven Horsford, D-Nev., said he wants the House to take up a bipartisan crypto tax bill before this Congress ends, after speaking with Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith, R-Mo., about its prospects.

"I talked to Chairman Smith earlier this week. He agreed that we have the opportunity to get our bipartisan bill through the House," Horsford said Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum.

Horsford said the bill should be brought up in the House before this Congress ends, but did not give a date for a vote.

The Digital Asset Tax Certainty Act, introduced by Smith, cleared the House Ways and Means Committee by 38-5 in September. The legislation would establish federal tax rules for digital assets. Horsford said Thursday that Smith's bill took in most of the provisions from the Digital Asset PARITY Act, which Horsford developed with Rep. Max Miller, R-Ohio.

Horsford also said he remained bullish on the Clarity Act, a crypto market structure bill, despite its stalling in the Senate ahead of the U.S. midterms, though he wanted ethics and transparency guardrails in the legislation, calling the GENIUS Act, Clarity Act, and PARITY Act a "three-legged stool" for digital asset policy.

Under Smith's committee-approved bill, a person using crypto to pay a qualifying network or transaction fee of no more than $10 would not recognize a gain or loss on that payment. The bill also extends wash-sale rules to traded digital assets.

Horsford said after the committee vote in September that the bill would tax mining and staking rewards as ordinary income but still did not settle when that income should be recognized.

#4996. DBS bets it can lose the deposit and keep the client

The Singapore bank earned a record $208 million in transaction services fees in the third quarter — now it’s helping companies turn deposits into stablecoins they can spend without it.

If a supplier wants USDC on a Sunday, an eligible company can now get Circle’s stablecoin directly from DBS.

That gives the bank a conversion option its client might otherwise take to a crypto exchange. If the treasurer needs to move dollars between the company’s Singapore and US accounts that weekend, DBS can also handle that through its new tokenized deposit link with JPMorgan.

Once the company holds USDC, its treasurer can pay the next invoice without involving DBS. For shareholders, the payoff depends on how often that treasurer comes back, what DBS earns when the money moves and how much operating cash the company leaves on deposit.

Related: Binance hacker moves nearly $600M in ETH, routes funds through THORChain, Chainflip

The Monetary Authority of Singapore issued 100 million Singapore dollars ($77.6 million) of four-week bills on Nov. 6, and the dealers paid in wholesale Singapore dollar central bank digital currency. Here DBS participated as a securities dealer. MAS plans to expand trials in 2027 to explore how institutions could use the bills as collateral.

DBS’s quarterly results put a figure on the business it has to defend. Transaction services generated a record 268 million Singapore dollars in fees in the third quarter, up 8% from a year earlier, before either November deal.

#4994. SEC Freezes Assets in $560 Million Crypto Trading Ponzi Scheme

The Securities and Exchange Commission has frozen the assets of Indianapolis-based Backward Hat Capital and its two co-founders, saying the firm ran a $560 million Ponzi scheme that told investors their money would be turned into crypto and used for AI-driven currency trading.

The agency said Backward Hat, co-founders Phillip Williams and Devin Johnson, Johnson’s firm Traders Point Capital and Joseph Anderson raised the money from more than 2,000 investors starting in July 2022. A federal court granted a temporary restraining order, an asset freeze and expedited discovery to halt what the SEC called an ongoing scheme.

Federal prosecutors in New York and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission brought parallel cases. The SEC and CFTC suits were filed in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Indiana, and the CFTC said the court has appointed Kevin B. Duff as temporary receiver to take control of the companies and the individual defendants’ assets.

According to the SEC’s complaint, Williams and Johnson sold limited partnership interests in private funds through a network of promoters, including to Williams’s former investment advisory clients. They promised a proprietary, “AI-driven” algorithm that supposedly returned at least 6% every month by trading foreign exchange.

Investor money, the pitch went, would first be converted into a crypto asset and then used for that arbitrage. The complaint does not name bitcoin as the asset. It says about $109 million of the roughly $210 million raised from July 2022 to September 2025 was sent to the fund’s Coinbase account and converted.

Of that, more than $56 million went to personal crypto accounts held by Williams, Johnson and a relief defendant, according to the complaint. About $40 million ended up on three platforms where some of it was used to trade other crypto assets, but little to no money was used to trade FX. After October 2025, the scheme raised about $350 million, and none of it was sent to Coinbase.

The SEC said the defendants made more than $250 million in Ponzi payments to earlier investors and moved more than $220 million to themselves. The money paid for exotic cars, expensive homes, private air travel and other businesses they owned, and helped feed Johnson’s gambling. Promoters took more than $50 million.

Anderson is accused of producing fake financial reports, including at least two “audit reports” and a “quality of earnings” report from a supposedly independent firm. The SEC said the documents falsely claimed the returns had been verified against bank and brokerage records. It said Anderson did no audit work, was not independent and had a financial stake in the scheme.

Prosecutors in Manhattan charged Williams and Johnson with securities fraud, conspiracy to commit wire fraud and conspiracy to commit securities fraud. The FBI arrested Williams in Kokomo, Indiana, and Johnson in Gilbert, Arizona, on Wednesday. Prosecutors put the total raised at more than $600 million.

“As alleged, Phillip Patrick Williams and Devin Allen Johnson promised to use investor funds for profitable foreign exchange currency trading,” said U.S. Attorney Jamie McDonald. “Williams and Johnson instead stole hundreds of millions of dollars from their investors for personal expenses and to pay off earlier investors in a Ponzi-like fashion.”

The CFTC put the Ponzi at more than $550 million from at least 2,100 participants and said defendants kept soliciting money through at least October 2026. Its complaint seeks restitution, disgorgement, civil penalties, trading bans and a permanent injunction. The SEC is seeking injunctions, disgorgement and civil penalties.

The case follows other recent enforcement actions tied to crypto-branded fraud. The SEC charged two companies in a $130 million crypto trading scheme last month, and New York sought a $1.37 billion judgment against the operators of the NovaTech Ponzi scheme. In August, the CFTC charged Goliath Ventures with a $400 million bitcoin fraud.

Anne McKinley, acting associate director of the SEC’s Chicago Regional Office, said the promise of “a low-risk, high rate of return on a purportedly complex investment should make all potential investors stop and think before investing their money.”

#4993. Wagyu’s XMR1 wrapped Monero plunges 99% after $13 million hack as founder promises refunds

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

PerpetualCow blames a hosting provider breach for the $13 million exploit and promises refunds as Wagyu suspends service.

Cross-chain swap and bridge platform Wagyu's wrapped Monero token XMR1 collapsed more than 99% on Friday after an attacker allegedly accessed its deployer wallet and dumped the stolen tokens on Hyperliquid.

XMR1 fell from nearly $500 to as low as $0.65. Blockchain tracker Lookonchain first reported that an attacker received 5.2 million XMR1 from the token's deployer wallet and sold a portion of them for USDC on Hyperliquid before using those funds to buy nearly 7,500 ETH.

The token is a wrapped version of Monero that can be used and redeemed on the Wagyu platform. Monero's actual XMR is unaffected by this incident.

The platform's pseudonymous founder, who goes by the moniker PerpetualCow, blamed the hack on a breach at the Icelandic hosting provider 1984.hosting, saying the XMR1 deployer key had been exposed.

"The XMR1 deployer key was held on an OLD server on there that I DELETED, but somehow the logs/files were still kept," Cow wrote on X.

Post-mortem of the $13m XMR hack: It's WORSE than I thought. Turns out that the entire hosting provider of https://t.co/aZzKk23eCg got hacked with everyone's data + internal server files today. The XMR1 deployer key was held on an OLD server on there that I DELETED, but somehow the logs/files were still kept. Lesson to learn here is that these hosting providers keep your data/logs even after you delete your server with them. Like I said before, everyone who lost money due to this will be refunded by me personally and Wagyu will re-launch at a later stage. Give me some time please.

Icelandic outlets DV and mbl.is reported on Friday that 1984 temporarily suspended its services following a cyberattack. However, this does not automatically confirm the founder's claims about bad actors accessing the retained keys.

Wagyu's website displayed a notice saying the service was "temporarily unavailable" at the time of publication.

"Everyone who lost money due to this will be refunded by me personally and Wagyu will re-launch at a later stage," PerpetualCow said in his post. He did not provide a timetable.

#4992. Bitcoin Breaks Below $60K as Crypto Fear Index Hits Worst Level Since February

Bitcoin Breaks Below $60K as Crypto Fear Index Hits Worst Level Since February

Bitcoin broke below $60,000 on Friday, plunging through a major psychological price level as the selloff gathered pace after Anthropic’s stock market debut.

The daily chart shows BTC at $59,703.72 , down 3.55% for the session after touching a low of $59,300.

The Fear and Greed Index chart dipped to 9, a reading classified as “extreme fear” and its lowest since February. For reference, the index was at 18 a week ago and a neutral 51 a month earlier.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The indicator tracks crypto market sentiment on a scale from zero, or maximum fear, to 100, or extreme greed, using market data rather than individual investor surveys. Its 2026 low was 5 on February 5, compared with a yearly high of 82 on August 26.

The selling went well beyond Bitcoin. Ethereum fell 4.24%, Solana lost 6.36%, BNB slid 4.38% and XRP declined 5.53% over the same period.

Forced selling added to the pressure. As Decrypt reported earlier Friday, roughly $727 million in leveraged crypto positions had been liquidated over 24 hours, about 86% of them bullish bets, while U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs lost $582.2 million on Thursday.

Spot fund withdrawals did not begin with Anthropic’s IPO. By Thursday, roughly $2.9 billion in U.S. Bitcoin ETF outflows for November, alongside higher Treasury yields and expectations of tighter Federal Reserve policy.

Anthropic made its Nasdaq debut Thursday at $111.50, up 34% from the $83 IPO price, before closing at $97.17. Friday brought another decline for the Claude developer, with the stock almost losing all the prime it gained after its IPO.

The listing raised $81.34 billion through the public offering, but enthusiasm for Anthropic shares did not carry over to the companies building the AI boom.

Friday’s market heatmap shows tech stock suffering in the middle of mostly green pastures. Nvidia down 3.78%, Broadcom is down 5.65%, AMD declining 5.06% and memory-chip maker Micron dropping 6.51%. Meta fell 2.21%, adding to pressure on companies spending heavily on AI infrastructure.

Other major stocks were doing better. Alphabet rose 2.81%, Microsoft gained 0.50% and Apple edged up 0.25%, while healthcare stocks Eli Lilly and Johnson & Johnson rose 1.21% and 1.78%, respectively. Walmart added 2.91% and Visa climbed 1.34%.

But it was not a uniform crash across the S&P 500. The tech-heavy Nasdaq was falling more sharply than the broader index, while pockets of healthcare, consumer, and financial shares remained green.

Bitcoin’s daily chart turned more decisively bearish after the break below $60,000. Its 50-day exponential moving average, or EMA, was below the 200-day EMA, a death-cross configuration, and spot prices were trading beneath both lines. That confirms sustained downside momentum rather than a brief intraday dip.

The relative strength index, or RSI, had fallen into the teens, far below the 30 threshold usually associated with oversold conditions. The average directional index, or ADX, climbed to 43.7, signaling a strong trend, with the chart’s directional readings favoring sellers. Oversold does not mean a bounce is guaranteed.

The longer-range chart puts the $60,000 breakdown in context. Bitcoin traded around this area several times in 2024 and returned to it during the mid-2026 retreat, making it a visible historical price zone as well as a round-number threshold.

With $60,000 giving way, the roughly $58,000–$59,000 support area near the mid-2026 lows comes into focus. If that zone fails, the next notable historical demand area is around $55,000–$56,000, followed by the $50,000 region marked by the deeper 2024 selloff.

For bulls, $60,000 is now the first level to hold. The next hurdle is $62,000–$62,500, followed by $65,000 and heavier resistance near $68,000–$70,000. A brief bounce back above $60,000 would not, by itself, reverse the bearish trend.

As of right now, Bitcoin had touched $59,300.13, confirming that sellers had pushed below the $60,000 support zone. The next test is whether that level becomes resistance on a rebound.

#4989. JPMorgan says bitcoin price correction widens gap to estimated $86,000 production cost

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

JPMorgan says the widening gap likely put more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin reserves.

Bitcoin's recent price drop has widened the gap between its market price and JPMorgan's estimated production cost of $86,000, likely putting more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin from their reserves.

Bitcoin was trading near $65,000 when JPMorgan analysts led by Nikolaos Panigirtzoglou published the report on Wednesday, about $21,000 below their central cost estimate. Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July, widening that gap to more than $26,000.

The analysts said miners likely added to the selling as the gap widened. Higher-cost miners may have sold their bitcoin reserves to raise cash, continue operating or fund a move into AI, they said.

"That said, we believe the gap could narrow as the hashrate declines and mining difficulty adjusts as unprofitable miners exit the network," the analysts wrote.

The analysts said crypto prices fell sharply over the past two weeks as tech stocks slid further and investors revised their expectations for Fed rates.

Spot bitcoin and ether ETFs, along with leveraged ETFs linked to Michael Saylor's Strategy, saw large outflows in both October and November, similar to May and June, the analysts said. Weekly data showed that the outflows were heavier in the latest two weeks than in the previous four weeks. Equity ETFs, on the other hand, saw inflows.

The analysts said retail investors likely drove most of the crypto ETF outflows because institutional positioning in futures markets largely held up. "Our CME futures positioning proxies suggest that bitcoin positioning has declined modestly, while ethereum positioning remained relatively elevated, holding up even better than bitcoin," the analysts said.

As for retail investors, they may have sold crypto ETFs to fund stock ETF purchases, seeing better opportunities to buy the dip in equities, according to the analysts. They also pointed to a decline in stablecoin market capitalization as a sign that "some capital has also left the crypto market altogether."

#4987. Wrapped Monero Token Collapses 99% After $13 Million Hack Blamed on Web Host

Wrapped Monero Token Collapses 99% After $13 Million Hack Blamed on Web Host

A wrapped version of Monero lost nearly all its value Friday after an attacker drained its deployer wallet and dumped the tokens on Hyperliquid.

XMR1, a token issued by cross-chain swap service Wagyu that represents Monero on Hyperliquid, fell more than 99% in roughly half an hour, from about $500 to around $1.

The account that deployed the token sent 5.2 million XMR1 to a newly created account early Friday, according to an analysis by onchain data firm Bitquery.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That account sold about 364,000 of the tokens to 388 buyers for $13.5 million, then converted the proceeds to ETH. At Bitquery’s last check, about 7,350 ETH sat in three wallets.

Wagyu’s pseudonymous founder, PerpetualCow, blamed a breach at 1984.hosting, an Icelandic web hosting provider.

He wrote on X that the XMR1 deployer key had been stored on an old server there that he had deleted, but that the host apparently kept its logs and files. “Lesson to learn here is that these hosting providers keep your data/logs even after you delete your server with them,” he wrote.

1984 ehf., the company behind the service, said it had suffered an extensive and serious cyberattack on its IT infrastructure, Icelandic outlet DV reported Friday, citing a joint statement with CERT-IS, Iceland’s national cybersecurity incident response team.

Early findings point to a well-organized and technically sophisticated attack, and the company said it couldn’t rule out that more systems were affected than it had confirmed. It temporarily shut down all services. The statement didn’t mention Wagyu or whether customer data was taken.

Turns out that the entire hosting provider of https://t.co/skXiWey4Fb got hacked with everyone's data + internal server files today.

The XMR1 deployer key was held on an OLD server on there that I DELETED, but…

— PerpetualCow.hl (@PerpetualCow) November 20, 2026

PerpetualCow said he would personally refund everyone who lost money and that Wagyu would relaunch later. “Give me some time please,” he wrote, but didn’t give a timeline.

Bitquery said onchain data alone can’t show whether the deployer’s key was stolen, and that it can’t see how much Monero backs the remaining XMR1, since the Monero side of the bridge isn’t visible.

The attacker’s account received its first funds from the crypto mixer Tornado Cash minutes before it was set up. The same deployer account still controls nearly all of ZEC1, a Zcash token. Wagyu also disclosed a separate data leak in October, saying at the time that funds were unaffected.

Wagyu surfaced earlier this month in ZachXBT’s tracing of a suspected $106 million theft from an unnamed European entity, in which part of the funds were converted to Monero through the service.

#4984. Binance hack laundering ramps up as $149 million in bitcoin mixed through coinjoins: Elliptic

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Attackers moved $860 million in ether from major wallets, swapping some into bitcoin before mixing it within hours.

Launderers allegedly linked to North Korea are now mixing tens of millions of dollars in bitcoin a day in a bid to make tracing the funds harder.

At least 2,310 BTC, worth around $149 million at the time, has been mixed through coinjoin transactions, with roughly 90% of that occurring since Tuesday, Elliptic said in a Friday update.

Coinjoins are a method of obfuscating the source of a bitcoin transaction by combining different users' transactions. This makes it harder to match senders' and recipients' coins.

This follows transfers of around 488,000 ETH, valued at $860 million, from five wallets. Most of this ether is now parked in new addresses, minus around $70 million that was swapped to bitcoin through THORChain. That BTC was subsequently mixed within hours.

The report's author, Tom Robinson, noted the coinjoins were organized by Kruw, a Wasabi Wallet coordinator. Coordinators arrange the transactions to be mixed but don't take custody of users' funds.

The 18,720 BTC stolen directly from Binance are still untouched. The bitcoins being mixed stem from swaps of other stolen assets like ETH, XRP and SOL.

Despite this, Elliptic said very little has likely been cashed out. The firm's latest tally puts about $2.35 billion in wallets linked to the theft, while $2.85 billion is frozen.

Separately, Binance said Wednesday that it obtained a $6.5 billion worldwide freeze order from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts against the attackers. Additional court orders give issuers and exchanges the green light to return stolen assets that they've already frozen.

Binance previously described the October breach as a monthslong campaign involving malicious software, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that gave attackers access to the exchange's wallet-signing systems.

#4982. Bitcoin Price Slips to Lows Not Seen Since 2024, Nasdaq Plunges 5%

The bitcoin price slid to near $58,300 on Friday afternoon, down about 6.2% over the past 24 hours, according to Look Into Bitcoin data, as a deepening selloff in chip and artificial intelligence stocks sent the Nasdaq down about 5% and pushed bitcoin just under its summer low.

Bitcoin broke below $60,000 late Friday morning, a few hours after this morning’s report had it near $61,500. Over the past 24 hours, the bitcoin price has traded between $57,655 and $62,562, a drop of about $3,880. Bitcoin is down about 25% in November, on pace for its worst month since June 2022.

Selling on Wall Street sped up through the afternoon. The Nasdaq Composite fell about 5% in late trading and the S&P 500 dropped about 3.5%. Yahoo Finance said the Nasdaq was on pace to close negative for the year, while the S&P 500 was poised to close in correction territory.

Chipmakers led the drop. Nvidia (NVDA) fell about 12.5% to near $128, and AMD, Broadcom and Micron also posted double-digit losses. Gold futures dropped nearly 5%. Treasury yields fell as traders rotated into bonds and defensive sectors such as consumer staples and healthcare, Reuters reported.

Anthropic (ANT) fell nearly 12% to around $86 on its first full day of trading, leaving it just above its $83 IPO price. The stock had closed its Thursday debut at $97.17 after raising $81.3 billion in the largest IPO of all time, CNBC reported.

Leveraged traders were forced out as the bitcoin price broke support. About $1.53 billion in crypto bets were wiped out over 24 hours, with long positions making up $1.4 billion of that total. Bitcoin alone accounted for around $540 million, while ether fell more than 8% to around $1,600.

Bitcoin funds have been sellers, too. Investors have pulled roughly $3.5 billion from U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs so far this month, including $582 million on Thursday. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) accounted for $541.1 million of Thursday’s withdrawals, its largest one-day outflow on record.

Rate worries are adding pressure. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent pushed back Friday on calls for a half-point Federal Reserve hike in December. Traders still saw a more than 98% chance of a quarter-point increase, and some strategists warned that further hikes could deepen the selloff.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said the drop looks driven more by the AI stock selloff than by problems inside crypto. Bitcoin has increasingly traded alongside other growth assets that depend on easy money, he said.

The dip to $57,655 briefly took bitcoin just under its summer low near $57,700. It is also on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, its longest losing streak since 2022.

Strategy (MSTR) fell about 8.4% to near $78.90, adding to the losses after the stock fell for a sixth straight day on Thursday. Coinbase (COIN) dropped about 5.5% to near $103. Robinhood (HOOD) lost about 9.6% and Circle (CRCL) fell about 7.9%.

Bitcoin miners fared worst. MARA Holdings (MARA) sank about 17% to near $5.17, IREN (IREN) fell about 17% and CleanSpark (CLSK) dropped about 16%. Riot Platforms (RIOT) lost about 8.4%, and Galaxy Digital (GLXY) fell about 9.4%. Many miners have expanded into AI data centers, tying their shares to the chip selloff.

The bitcoin price is now down more than 50% from its record above $126,000, set in October 2025.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $58,300.

#4980. Google Gives Gemini Control of Mac Apps, But Its New AI Agent Comes With Risks

Google Gives Gemini Control of Mac Apps, But Its New AI Agent Comes With Risks

Google wants Gemini to do more than answer questions on your Mac.

The company launched a computer-use feature Thursday that lets its AI assistant open applications, navigate menus, fill out forms, and move information between local files and websites on a user's behalf.

“Starting today, computer use in the Gemini app for Mac helps you get multi-step tasks done across your local files, desktop apps, and the web,” Google announced. “Just describe what you need, and Gemini handles the clicking, typing, menus, and forms for you.”

The new capability runs in what Google calls an isolated background window, leaving the computer's main display and cursor available for the owner. Users can watch the agent's actions through a live preview inside the Gemini chat and interrupt a task if it goes off course.

Google's examples include extracting shipping details from PDF documents and entering them into online forms, editing batches of photos, and building presentations from dashboard data. The company also suggests researching prices and preparing resale listings based on pictures stored on a Mac.

Those jobs involve a different kind of AI interaction than asking a chatbot to write instructions. Gemini is supposed to operate the software itself, carrying out the clicks and keystrokes that would otherwise fall to the user.

The feature is rolling out in English in the United States to adult users with personal paid Google accounts. It requires an Apple Silicon Mac running macOS Sequoia 15.0 or later; work and school accounts are not supported at launch.

To activate it, users enable computer use in the Mac app, open Task mode, and prompt Gemini with @Computer use. According to Google's support documentation, the assistant asks for permission before controlling a new app, with options to deny access, allow it once, or allow it on an ongoing basis.

Users can create lists of allowed and blocked apps, monitor the virtual screen, and stop a task. Google says sensitive actions require confirmation before the agent proceeds, though an app marked "always allow" can be accessed again without another app-level prompt.

Computer use adds a local dimension to Google's agent strategy. Decrypt previously covered Gemini Spark, the company's cloud-based task agent, while competitors have developed desktop-control tools including OpenAI's Codex, Anthropic's Claude, and Kimi Work.

The separate window is also a practical distinction: an agent that commandeers the visible cursor can make a computer difficult to use during a long task. Google's approach lets the person keep working while Gemini handles another workflow.

A background window should not be confused with a guarantee that files or accounts are isolated from risk. Google's own documentation warns that the agent can reach active sessions in desktop applications, encounter authentication codes displayed on screen, and take actions that some apps cannot reverse.

In that case, any error is the user’s fault, not Google’s.

It also warns about prompt injection, an attack in which malicious instructions hidden in a document or website persuade an AI system to do something its user never requested. With permission to operate multiple apps, Gemini could encounter hostile content in one program and act on it in another.

Google offers optional on-demand file backups but says they cannot always be created. Where they work, the temporary copies remain available for 24 hours or until the next prompt, and some applications may prevent their content from being backed up at all.

The launch also comes three days after Apple introduced tighter access controls in the macOS 27.3 beta. Those changes give users more ways to restrict powerful permissions to specific apps or folders and to require fresh approval each time access is requested.

#4979. Rep. Casten seeks to curb prediction market insider trading with DOJ whistleblower program

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Reps. Casten and Lieu introduced a bill that would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously.

Rep. Sean Casten, who could have a top spot in Congress next year, is clamping down on insider trading in prediction markets by incentivizing people to report information anonymously to the Justice Department, amid high-profile incidents involving governmental officials and military personnel.

On Friday, Casten and Rep. Ted Lieu introduced the Policing Insider Trading in Prediction Markets Act, which would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously about fraud in prediction markets and could potentially be compensated if the information results in a successful enforcement action.

"Prediction markets have a serious insider trading problem, and the current Administration is engaged in corruption of epic proportions," Casten said in a statement. "By encouraging whistleblowers to come forward and report wrongdoing, we can take an important step in cracking down on insider trading to restore accountability and public confidence in the U.S. rule of law."

The news was earlier reported by Politico.

Over the past year, certain bets on prediction markets have drawn scrutiny from both Republicans and Democrats. On Thursday, the Senate passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination and war.

Insider trading on these platforms has also emerged as an ongoing concern, particularly following reports that an active-duty U.S. Army soldier used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of the capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. Subsequent cases have raised further concerns about government officials exploiting access to nonpublic information for financial gain through prediction markets, including allegations involving the White House's former teleprompter operator.

Political candidates have likewise faced allegations of insider trading on these platforms.

With Democrats now taking control of Congress in January, prediction markets are likely to garner more scrutiny. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who is slated to chair the Senate Banking Committee, has said she would hone in on prediction markets. On the House side, it is not yet clear who will lead the House Financial Services Committee. Rep. Maxine Waters had been expected to take on that role, but has since been challenged by Casten.

#4975. US Senate advances prediction market restrictions with DEATH BETS Act

The Senate-approved bill would ban prediction market contracts tied to war, assassination and death amid concerns over potential insider trading on military events.

The US Senate passed legislation Thursday that would prohibit federally regulated prediction markets from offering contracts tied to war, terrorism, assassination and events primarily determined by a person’s death, sending the bill to the House for consideration.

The DEATH BETS Act, introduced by Democratic Sen. Adam Schiff in March, passed by voice vote on Thursday after lawmakers adopted an amendment by unanimous consent. The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act to prohibit CFTC-registered exchanges from listing or clearing contracts tied to the specified events.

The legislation also covers contracts referencing US military operations and gives the Commodity Futures Trading Commission authority to identify similar activities that would fall under the ban. Contracts would also be prohibited if, at the time of listing, they are reasonably expected to be settled primarily based on an individual’s death.

The bill was first introduced in March amid insider-trading concerns after six Polymarket traders reportedly earned $1 million betting on the timing of US strikes against Iran. Several wagers were placed just hours before the first reported explosions in Tehran, raising suspicions that traders may have had access to nonpublic information.

When introducing the bill, Schiff warned that allowing traders to profit from violent events could encourage the misuse of classified information, threaten national security and create incentives for violence.

The bill now moves to the House of Representatives, where it must pass before being sent to President Donald Trump for his signature.

Cathie Wood Bets $91M on Anthropic IPO While Paring Crypto Exposure

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

During the same session, ARK trimmed two crypto-linked positions, selling about $2.9 million of the 3iQ Ethereum staking ETF and $1.2 million of the 3iQ Solana staking ETF, a combined $4.1 million, from its ARKW and ARKF funds.

Anthropic’s debut was one of the year’s most anticipated listings. The company priced its IPO at $83 per share, and the stock surged before cooling, closing its first day at $97.17, up 17% from the offering price but down sharply from its intraday high, with nearly 850 million shares changing hands.

The timing underscores the crosscurrents buffeting both sectors. ARK’s crypto trims landed as digital assets slid, with Ethereum down about 8% on the day near $1,620, part of a broader selloff that has pressured crypto funds.

For Wood, long one of crypto’s most prominent institutional champions, the week’s trades suggest a tactical tilt toward the AI boom even as she keeps a foot firmly in digital assets, buying crypto stocks one day and trimming crypto ETFs the next.

#4968. Chainflip’s FLIP drops 34% after attacker drains $12 million from vaults with suspected key compromise

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

An attacker emptied the Ethereum and Bitcoin vaults of cross-chain DEX Chainflip, looting about $4qw million in crypto and sending it to Tornado Cash, apparently by using a legitimate signing key.

Cross-chain decentralized exchange Chainflip Chainflip (FLIP) paused its protocol on Saturday morning after security firms detected an attack which looted about $4.86 million in ETH and stablecoins from its Ethereum-based vault, apparently using a valid signature from the protocol's own signing key.

Chainflip later disclosed that 122.86 BTC was also stolen from its Bitcoin vault, bringing the total value of stolen crypto to about $12 million. Chainflip has offered a 10% bounty to the attacker, which it says it may be able to identify based on metadata traces they left behind.

"We're aware of a potential exploit affecting Chainflip vaults," the protocol shared on X. "As a precaution, the protocol is being put into full safe mode. Deposits, withdrawals, swaps and quoting are disabled."

Chainflip's FLIP token plummeted about 34% following the 2:10 a.m. ET exploit, per The Block's Chainflip price page. The token's market capitalization dropped from about $41.6 million to $27.1 million as of publication time.

Security firm Decurity first shared details of the attack on X through its Defimon Alerts bot. The alert noted Chainflip's Ethereum vault was drained of "its entire liquid balance," making up 520.2 ETH, 3.25M USDC and 758K USDT worth around $4.86 million at the time. The alert said that rather than exploiting a smart contract bug, the attacker apparently obtained access to the validator network's aggregate signing key, meaning the secret code that authorizes transfers.

The attacker launched the hack through a fresh wallet, and later sent nearly all the proceeds through mixing protocol Tornado Cash, per security firm AMLBot. "The laundering was executed with high-speed automation," AMLBot stated, noting that the stablecoins moved too fast for USDC issuer Circle and USDT issuer Tether to freeze the tokens, as the whole attack took place in under 10 minutes.

Chainflip has not said how the attacker may have compromised the key, which is jointly controlled by the protocol's 150 validators and rotated every three days; the last rotation happened on Nov. 18 at 4:53 p.m. ET, about two and a half days before the exploit. No one validator is supposed to have access to the full key; rather, each holds a piece and 100 out of 150 are required to sign transactions.

Blockchain data firm Bitquery noted in its investigation that Chainflip's key changed about an hour and 45 minutes after the theft. The timing suggests Chainflip rotated the compromised key to prevent further thefts, though Bitquery noted that no conclusions can be drawn from timing alone.

In another quirk Bitquery noted, the attacker's transaction carried a strange nonce, or a unique number that every Chainflip withdrawal is stamped with. That number, each of which is only used once, usually increments by one to prevent anyone from replaying old transactions, but rather than having a nonce around 315,000 like other transactions around that time, the attacker's nonce was a 39-digit number.

That suggests the approval wasn't made through Chainflip's normal system, which would have handed out the next number on the counter, but that the attacker somehow produced a valid signature outside that system. Bitquery noted that the drain was the only one of 299,677 signatures since 2023 that broke the count.

Chainflip later said the attacker also took 122.86 BTC from its Bitcoin vault, bringing total losses to about $12 million. A separate transaction moved about 10.5 BTC from a previous Chainflip vault address to a new address about two hours after Chainflip paused the protocol. That transfer is not among the assets Chainflip listed as stolen.

Chainflip previously suffered an exploit on Sept. 12 that saw an attacker abuse a flaw in how Chainflip processed Tron transaction memos to double-process six withdrawals on the Tron network.

The protocol's validators later voted to reimburse the affected liquidity providers (LPs) using the protocol's "surplus funds," which Chainflip described as "a combination of many things. It is both excess funds held by the protocol through gas fee interactions, assets sent to Chainflip in error that the protocol could not automatically return, and other sometimes so far unexplained surpluses that have built up in the vaults since launch."

Chainflip has not said whether it plans to reimburse the victims of the current hack. Chainflip did not immediately respond to a request for comment from The Block.

Updated Nov. 22 at 1:00 p.m. ET with Chainflip's disclosure of the bitcoin theft and a link to our updated reporting.

#4966. SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

Sam Bankman-Fried had an eventful Thursday—at least on the internet.

Anthropic went public, Netflix released “The Altruists,” and Crypto Twitter imagined the fortune his collapsed exchange might have made by keeping its AI investment.

At Anthropic’s November 19 closing price of $97.17, FTX’s former shares would have been worth $43.28 billion. At the $111.50 opening trade, their theoretical value would have reached $49.66 billion.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That money never belonged personally to Bankman-Fried. The bankruptcy estate sold the shares to repay creditors after FTX’s 2022 collapse.

The former CEO is serving a 25-year sentence for fraud after using FTX customer funds to make investments and cover losses, plus got hit with $11 billion in forfeiture—a fraction of what his single Anthropic investment would have returned today.

A company linked to Alameda, FTX’s sister company, committed $500 million to Anthropic in October 2021. The money converted into roughly 44.54 million preferred shares during the AI startup’s 2022 Series B, about 13.6% of the company.

Bankruptcy administrators sold the position in two batches in 2024 for approximately $1.34 billion. Buyers included an Abu Dhabi-linked fund and Jane Street, Bankman-Fried’s former employer, as filings showed.

At Thursday’s closing price that investment would have produced a total of nearly $43.28 billion—roughly 32 times what creditors’ administrators actually collected.

Sadly, that fortune is hypothetical. Later financing diluted FTX’s percentage ownership, the shares carried restrictions, and the estate needed liquidity to compensate customers who lost money when FTX went belly up. A judge approved the sale in February 2024.

Tiffany Fong, who rose to Twitter fame after interviewing SBF just before he was arrested, shared an AI-generated video of a crying Bankman-Fried drinking in a car alongside the $40 billion-plus calculation. Another account posted a crying-man meme about him watching the listing from prison.

Sam Bankman-Fried’s $500 million investment in Anthropic would now be worth over $40 billion.

The FTX estate sold it for only $1.34 billion during bankruptcy. pic.twitter.com/H2tua0D4V0

— Tiffany Fong (@TiffanyFong) November 20, 2026

Vietnamese commentator Yuki Sato likened the predicament to selling a house to settle debts before its value increased 100-fold. French poster Mehdi lamented SBF’s short-time focus.

FTX creditor advocate Sunil Kavuri reminded followers that customer deposits had funded the original investment and that the estate sold it to repay customers. Separate Anthropic investments tied to Caroline Ellison and Nishad Singh were handled through government forfeiture, so not the same estate sale, but still related in Crypto Twitter’s collective mind.

FTX customers funds used to invest in Series B at $2.5bn valuation

SBF invested $500m in Anthropic using FTX customer deposits in Series B (Valuation: $2.5bn)7.8% stake was sold for $1.3bn in 2024

Nishad Singh ($40m) and Caroline Ellison ($10m)…

— Sunil (FTX Creditor Champion) (@sunil_trades) November 19, 2026

FTX and Alameda’s portfolio extended far beyond Anthropic. A leaked investment spreadsheet showed roughly $5.4 billion allocated to around 500 bets. These are some of the most important ones:

Alameda put $200,000 into Anysphere, maker of the AI coding tool. The estate sold the stake for the same amount in 2023. SpaceX acquired the company at a $60 billion valuation in 2026. If the original holding was about 5%, its undiluted value would be near $3 billion; subsequent dilution makes that an upper-bound mark.

The estate sold roughly 25 million to 30 million locked SOL tokens near $64 each in 2024, Bloomberg reported. At SOL’s January 2025 high of $293.31, that tranche would have been worth a bit less than $9 billion.

Bankman-Fried’s vehicle spent about $648 million on Robinhood shares in 2022. Authorities seized 55.27 million shares, which Robinhood repurchased for $605.7 million in 2023. At the stock’s 2025 intraday high of $153.86, that block would have been worth approximately $8.5 billion.

Alameda’s $1.15 billion Genesis Digital investment would imply around $290 million at a 2026 transaction valuation. By contrast, $40 million in drone maker BRINC could mark near $109 million at a funding estimate, and $20 million in Anchorage Digital about $31 million at its 2026 valuation.

A 461-position reconstruction puts $5.32 billion in total investments at roughly $59.5 billion on today’s valuation for those companies and assets—about $54.2 billion in hypothetical gains. The model assumes all investments were held and imputes values for illiquid positions.

That’s good for a 1,018% return, and proof SBF knew how to pick his bets. But even the best investments can't justify gambling with other people's money without their consent. He made that bet, too—and lost.

#4964. Bitcoin falls below $60K for first time since July amid broader market selloff

Bitcoin fell nearly 6% as US stocks declined, with the Nasdaq dropping more than 2% and Anthropic shares losing almost 10%.

Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July, extending its recent losses amid a broader selloff in technology stocks and other risk assets.

Bitcoin (BTC) had fallen to $58,811 before recovering slightly to trade around $59,503, down 5.6% over the past 24 hours as of 16:52 UTC on Friday, according to CoinGecko data. The cryptocurrency has fallen 13.8% over the past week and nearly 30% over the past month.

The decline came amid a broader selloff in US equities. The Nasdaq Composite closed down 5.28% at 22,617.73, while the S&P 500 fell 3.69% to 6,910.77 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

Shares of Anthropic (ANT), which made its Nasdaq debut on Thursday, closed down 8.50% at $88.91 amid concerns over valuations and spending in the artificial intelligence sector.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds recorded $582.3 million in net outflows on Thursday, up from $306.5 million the previous day, according to Farside Investors. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) accounted for $541.1 million of Thursday’s withdrawals.

Bitcoin’s decline appears to be driven more by the broader selloff in artificial intelligence stocks than by problems specific to the cryptocurrency market, according to James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares.

Butterfill told Cointelegraph that Bitcoin has increasingly traded alongside other liquidity-sensitive growth assets, with deteriorating sentiment toward AI stocks spilling into crypto as investors reduce risk. The prospect of interest rates remaining elevated has made the broader selloff more severe, he added.

The Federal Reserve held its benchmark interest rate at 3.75% to 4.00% in October, following a quarter-point increase in September. Traders on Polymarket were pricing in a 94% chance of another rate hike by the Fed’s next policy decision on Dec. 9, as of 17:19 UTC on Friday.

Polymarket traders price in 94% chance of another Fed rate hike in 2026. Source: Polymarket

Despite Bitcoin falling below $60,000, Butterfill said the decline has not fundamentally changed his medium-term outlook.

Butterfill said Bitcoin could face further short-term losses if the AI selloff deepens, but should benefit relatively quickly if technology stocks stabilize.

Alexander Blume, founder and CEO of digital asset investment firm Two Prime, told Cointelegraph that macroeconomic instability, interest rates, inflation and a lack of Bitcoin-specific catalysts were contributing to the decline.

However, Blume suggested that selling pressure may be nearing an end.

#4963. Cyber investigating suspected $1 million bridge exploit as Upbit, Bithumb freeze transfers

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Upbit and Bithumb froze CYBER deposits and withdrawals as tokens pulled from the Ethereum bridge for Binance Labs-backed firm Cyber, formerly CyberConnect, were sold for about $968,000 in ETH.

Cyber, the social-focused Layer 2 network formerly known as CyberConnect, said Saturday that it is investigating "unexpected minting" of its CYBER CyberConnect (CYBER) token on Solana, which was followed by a transfer of CYBER out of its bridge's Ethereum lockbox. South Korean exchanges Upbit and Bithumb suspended CYBER deposits and withdrawals shortly after noon ET, citing a suspected security incident.

In an X post at 1:25 p.m. ET, Cyber said its team is checking which assets and bridge routes are affected. It asked users to hold off on using the CYBER bridge or depositing CYBER to exchanges, and said they don't need to migrate tokens, connect wallets or sign anything.

Upbit's caution notice linked two transactions. In the first, 5,213,000 CYBER was minted on Solana at 11:13 a.m. ET. Two minutes later, the same amount left CyberTokenAdapter, the Ethereum contract that holds CYBER for the token's LayerZero bridge, and went to a wallet that had been funded for the first time less than an hour earlier.

That wallet and a second one sold most of the tokens for ETH over the next hour through KyberSwap and MetaMask Swaps, mostly in clips of 150,000 CYBER at a time. At 12:40 p.m. ET, they sent 575.29 ETH to a fresh address, where it was still sitting as of publication. Another roughly 412,000 CYBER went to three addresses that block explorers haven't labeled.

CYBER fell about 8% to $0.2024 as the tokens were sold, before recovering to about $0.213, according to The Block's Cyber price page as of publication time. Bithumb said CYBER trading would continue during the freeze, and Upbit issued a trading caution rather than halting trading.

Cyber started out as CyberConnect, a social graph protocol that raised a $10 million seed round co-led by Multicoin Capital and Sky9 Capital in 2021. The CYBER token launched through Binance Launchpool in August 2023, and Binance Labs, now known as YZi Labs, later made a follow-on investment in the project. In May 2024, CyberConnect rebranded as Cyber and launched an Ethereum Layer 2 network for social apps built on Optimism's OP Stack, spinning off its Link3 app as a separate brand.

Earlier this month, Cyber said it had bought back about $13 million worth of CYBER over the past year. The Block has reached out to Cyber for further comment.
